Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
IntraBio Ltd. (Applicant) submitted this new drug application (NDA) for levacetylleucine, an
N-acetyl-L-leucine drug product, also referred to by the company development code IB1001
(tradename Aqneursa), seeking approval of this small molecule as a treatment of Niemann-Pick
disease type C (NPC).
Niemann-Pick disease type C is a rare and serious neurodegenerative lysosomal disorder caused
by defective intracellular lipid trafficking and metabolism. As a result, patients with NPC have
progressive accumulation of several lipid species in affected cells in the nervous system and
several visceral organs. NPC is neurodegenerative, most commonly impacting ambulation, fine
motor skills, speech, swallowing, and cognition in addition to visceral symptoms. After the
aforementioned neurological symptoms, ataxia is one of the most common neurological
symptoms reported. This NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline
recommends approval of levacetylleucine for the treatment of the neurological manifestations of
NPC in adults and pediatric patients weighing at least 15 kg. The signatory authority for this
application concurs and recommends approval for this indication.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness of levacetylleucine for NPC was established using data
from one adequate and well-controlled trial and confirmatory evidence (CE). For full details,
refer to Section 1.2.
The available clinical safety data show that levacetylleucine is safe for its intended use. Common
adverse reactions include dysphagia, upper respiratory tract infections, abdominal pain and
vomiting. Aggravation of pre-existing rosacea was also reported as possibly related to treatment
during the trial. These risks observed are deemed acceptable with appropriate product labeling
and routine pharmacovigilance.
Data from dose-ranging nonclinical embryofetal development studies indicates that
levacetylleucine is fetotoxic (decrease in fetal weight, increase in resorptions, and
post-implantation loss) and causes external and skeletal malformation in both rodents and nonrodents. The embryo-fetal risks are adequately communicated through labeling. As described in
Section 2.2, we conclude that the improvement in neurological function observed with use of
levacetylleucine outweighs the risks when levacetylleucine is used as recommended in the
approved labeling.

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) of levacetylleucine for NPC was established using
data from one adequate and well-controlled trial and confirmatory evidence. The adequate and
3
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
well-controlled trial, Trial IB1001-301, demonstrated a clinically meaningful improvement in
neurological function that reached numerical statistical significance in children and adults with
NPC aged 5 years and older. With a p-value of <0.005 and a clinically meaningful magnitude of
improvement demonstrated for the primary endpoint in the treatment arm compared to placebo,
the review team considered the results of this trial highly persuasive.
CE is comprised of both clinical data and nonclinical mechanistic data that support the results of
Trial IB1001-301. The clinical data include the results of an open-label study of levacetylleucine
in patients with NPC, Trial IB1001-201. Thirty-three patients with NPC were enrolled in the
trial, which consisted of a 2-week baseline period, a 6-week treatment period, and a 6-week
washout period. The rater-blinded primary endpoint, clinical impression of change in severity
(CI-CS), measured each subject’s change in performance on their primary anchor test (a timed
subtest of the spinocerebellar ataxia functional index, either the 8-Meter Walk Test or the 9-Hole
Peg Test of the dominant hand) between the beginning and end of the treatment and washout
periods. Improvement in the CI-CS was observed in the treatment period and deterioration
during the washout period. While Trial IB1001-201 was an open-label study, the methodology of
blinding the CI-CS raters to time and treatment assignment increases the interpretability of the
resulting scores by reducing the influence of knowledge of treatment assignment. The CI-CS
results are supported by the timed spinocerebellar ataxia functional index subtests and indicate
either improvement or stability in the treatment period, and either deterioration or stability in the
washout period. Consequently, the Agency concludes that Trial IB1001-201 provided sufficient
supportive evidence that the subjects’ performance on the selected measures of gait and fine
motor function is comparatively favorable in the treatment period.
The supportive nonclinical mechanistic data that contributed to CE include in vitro and in vivo
data in (1) wildtype and NPC1-deficient Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) (Npc1-/- CHO) cells; (2)
fibroblasts derived from patients with NPC; and (3) Npc1-/- mice, all of which support the
potential for levacetylleucine treatment to slow disease progression in patients with NPC.
Published data suggest that treatment of Npc1-/- CHO cells with levacetylleucine may modestly
reduce accumulation of sphingosine, glycosphingolipids (GSLs), and cholesterol and may reduce
lysosomal volume compared to untreated Npc1-/- CHO cells. Additionally, in vitro treatment of
fibroblasts from patients with NPC with severe clinical presentation suggest that levacetylleucine
may reduce lysosomal volume in these cells. Published data from studies in NPC1-null mice
(Npc1-/- mice) treated with levacetylleucine reported a slight reduction in GSLs in brain and
cerebellum; improvements in ataxia, gait, and mobility; reduction in neuroinflammation in the
cerebellum and a small increase in lifespan compared to untreated Npc1-/- mice. These published
nonclinical studies suggest that amelioration of biochemical (sphingosine, GSLs and
cholesterol), cellular (lysosomal volume, neuroinflammation) and functional (ataxia, gait)
changes in nonclinical NPC models (in vitro and in vivo) upon treatment with levacetylleucine
may provide plausible mechanistic support for the effectiveness of levacetylleucine when
administered to patients with NPC. The demonstration of improvement in neurological function
(ataxia, gait, stance, sitting, and speech) in subjects with NPC in Trial IB1001-201, in
combination with the supportive nonclinical mechanistic data derived from published literature,
constitute the CE for this application. Refer to Section 6.3.3 for details.

Conclusions and Reasons

Niemann-Pick disease type C (NPC) is a rare, autosomal recessive
neurodegenerative disorder caused by mutations in the genes NPC1 or
NPC2. Impaired NPC protein function results in impaired intracellular
trafficking and metabolism, causing accumulation of unesterified cholesterol
and glycosphingolipids.
The primary manifestations and rate of disease progression is
heterogenous. NPC can present with severe hepatic failure and/or
respiratory failure in infancy up to the age of 2 years or with primarily
progressive neurological degeneration including cognitive impairment,
gross and fine motor delays, ataxia, psychiatric symptoms, vertical
supranuclear palsy, dystonia, and epilepsy.
Infants with systemic disease usually die in infancy. Across all types, the
median age of death is 13 years (range 0.1 to 69 years). Death is most
commonly due to respiratory failure in the setting of infection and/or
aspiration. While patients who are diagnosed during adulthood may survive
longer, they become fully dependent for supportive measures/ADLs

NPC is a serious and life-limiting
neurodegenerative disease that affects both
children and adults and leads to premature
death.

Only very recently has one therapy been approved in the United States for
the treatment of NPC. The current standard of care is primarily supportive
management of individual disease manifestations. Zavesca (miglustat) is
approved in the European Union, Canada, and Japan for the treatment of
progressive neurological manifestations in adult and pediatric patients with
NPC. In the United States, miglustat is approved for other indications
including Gaucher disease. Off-label use is considered standard of care in
adult and pediatric patients with NPC. Despite widespread use of miglustat,
there is continued disease progression, thus there is an unmet need for
approved therapies.

While there is one very recently approved
drug to treat the neurological manifestations
of NPC, there remains a need for additional
therapeutic options for patients diagnosed
with NPC, a devastating disease.
Because concomitant miglustat use is
common for patients with NPC, the impact of
this concomitant exposure on efficacy and
safety was considered during this review.

The observed treatment difference is clinically
meaningful to patients with NPC and/or their
caregivers.
The results of the adequate and wellcontrolled trial were highly persuasive. These
results, in combination with the additional
clinical and nonclinical data that both support

benefit. The data support the use of
levacetylleucine alone or with background

•

Aqneursa (levacetylleucine, IB1001) is a modified amino acid administered
orally and intended for the treatment of the neurological symptoms of NPC.
The efficacy of levacetylleucine was evaluated in an adequate and wellcontrolled trial (Trial IB1001-301) in children and adults with NPC. Changes
in neurological symptoms as measured by a functional Scale for
Assessment and Rating of Ataxia (fSARA) assessment of gait, stance,
sitting, and speech were compared in subjects randomly assigned to
treatment sequences to receive levacetylleucine followed by placebo or
receive placebo followed by levacetylleucine. Most of the subjects (85%) in
the trial used miglustat at baseline.
The estimated mean fSARA score was 5.1 when subjects were treated with
Aqneursa and 5.6 when subjects were treated with placbo. The estimated
treatment difference in mean fSARA scores was -0.45 (95% confidence
interval [CI]: -0.70, -0.19), favoring levacetylleucine and meeting numerical
statistical significance (p=0.0009). This level of change on the fSARA is
expected to affect how patients with NPC feel and function. The subgroup
analyses showed a consistent treatment difference, including for the
subgroup of subjects not on background miglustat.
Confirmatory evidence (CE) of effectiveness is derived from the phase 2
open-label trial with a rater blinded primary endpoint, IB1001-201, which
showed improvement in subject’s performance on either 8MWT or 9HPT-D
during the treatment period, and deterioration during the washout period
(Hodges-Lehmann estimate:1.0, 90% CI: 0.25, 1.75).
Additional supportive evidence is derived from published mechanistic
studies showing that levacetylleucine may reduce accumulation of lipids
(sphingosine, glycosphingolipds, cholesterol) and reduce lysosomal volume
in Npc1-deficient Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cells in vitro. In patientderived cells, levacetylleucine was reported to reduce lysosomal volume. In
Npc1-/- mice, levacetylleucine was reported to slightly reduce GSLs in brain
and cerebellum and to reduce ataxia, leading to improved gait and mobility.
Levacetylleucine was also reported to reduce neuroinflammation in
cerebellum and slightly improve lifespan. The published data described
above are supportive of the potential for levacetylleucine to reduce aspects
of NPC pathophysiology and is consistent with results from clinical trials.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
NPC is a rare and progressive neurovisceral disorder caused by mutations in the genes NPC1 or
NPC2 (Patterson 1993). These mutations result in impaired intracellular trafficking and
accumulation of unesterified cholesterol and GSLs within the late endosomes and lysosomes of
affected cells in the brain and other tissues. NPC is always progressive, but the primary
manifestations, severity, and rate of progression are variable. Individuals who develop the
symptoms of NPC in early childhood (after the age of 2 years) have a primarily
neurodegenerative course with progressive motor and cognitive impairment, supranuclear gaze
palsy, dysphagia, dysarthria, ataxia, and epilepsy, ultimately progressing to respiratory failure
and death.
There is currently only one very recently approved treatment for NPC. The current standard of
care is primarily supportive, however the substrate reducing therapy Zavesca (miglustat) is
approved in the European Union, Canada, and Japan for the treatment of NPC and off-label use
is considered standard of care in the United States. Despite widespread use of miglustat, NPC
continues to be progressive and life-limiting. Therefore, treatment and cure of NPC continue to
represent unmet needs.
The benefit of levacetylleucine is based on comparison of functional Scale for Assessment and
Rating of Ataxia scores in 60 subjects aged 5 to 67 years with NPC and at least one neurological
symptom randomized either to levacetylleucine or placebo for 12 weeks. The estimated
treatment difference in mean functional Scale for Assessment and Rating of Ataxia scores at the
end of week 12 was -0.45 (95% confidence interval: -0.70, -0.19), favoring the levacetylleucine
arm and meeting numerical statistical significance (p<0.001). CE of effectiveness is derived from
a separate phase 2 rater blinded open-label trial (Trial IB1001-201) and nonclinical
pharmacology studies (in vitro and in vivo) which are considered supportive of the overall
package of CE.
While no subjects under 5 years old were enrolled, extrapolation of efficacy to children who are
under 5 years old using pharmacokinetic data are supported by the similar disease
pathophysiology and predominant clinical manifestations in children with NPC with disease
onset after infancy. Similarly, a limited amount of extrapolation of clinical safety to children
under 5 years old and weight at least 15 kg was reasonable given the favorable benefit/risk
profile, the similar disease pathophysiology across the age spectrum, the ability to utilize
pharmacokinetic data for dose selection, and the lack of anticipated differential safety risk.
While there is a limited amount of safety data in younger children, the available data support that
levacetylleucine is safe for its intended use in adults and pediatric patients >15 kg and the overall
benefit/risk is favorable. Common adverse reactions include dysphagia, abdominal pain, and
vomiting. Aggravation of pre-existing rosacea was also reported as possibly related to treatment
during the trial. These risks can be adequately mitigated via product labeling and further
evaluated during routine pharmacovigilance.
Safety concerns from nonclinical studies include embryo-fetal toxicity. Uncertainties include the
lack of juvenile toxicity studies, fertility studies, and embryofetal, prenatal and postnatal
development studies, and carcinogenicity studies. These studies will be conducted as
postmarketing requirements.

Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Given that NPC is a serious and life-limiting rare disease with only one recently approved drug
to treat the neurological manifestations of NPC and a remaining unmet need for additional
therapeutic options, the review team has determined the benefit-risk of levacetylleucine to be
favorable for the treatment of adults and pediatric patients weighing at least 15 kg.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Niemann-Pick disease type C (NPC) is an autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder caused
by mutations in the genes NPC1 (95%) or NPC2 (5%) (Patterson 1993). NPC is a rare disease
that has an estimated prevalence of one to three cases per million in the United States (US),
although this is thought to be an underestimate given the heterogeneity of this disease and
likelihood of missed diagnoses in patients with milder phenotypes. Estimated incidences in
different populations around the world have ranged from 1:90,000 to 1:120,000 live births
(Wassif et al. 2016) (Labrecque et al. 2021).
NPC1 and NPC2 are important for late endosomal and lysosomal lipid transport and
homeostasis. Their loss of function impedes cellular lipid trafficking and causes the
accumulation of unesterified cholesterol, glucosylceramide, and gangliosides in the late
endosomes and lysosomes of affected cells (Tang et al. 2010). The accumulation of cholesterol
and other lipid species in peripheral tissues (mainly the liver and spleen) and in neurons and
other non-neuronal cells in the central nervous system is thought to ultimately lead to the
devastating and progressive neurovisceral symptoms that are the hallmarks of NPC via
downstream processes that are not fully understood.
NPC is always progressive, but the primary manifestations and prognosis are heterogenous,
ranging from a rapidly progressive neonatal form to an adult-onset form that is characterized by
a chronic neurodegenerative disease course. NPC is typically categorized into age-dependent
phenotypes, recognizing that they likely represent a continuum rather than distinct phenotypes.
The four phenotypes described are 1) early infantile, with perinatal onset to the age of 2 years,
(2) late infantile, with onset between the ages of 2 and 6 years, (3) juvenile, with onset between
the ages of 6 and 15 years, and (4) adolescent/adult, with onset after the age of 15 years. The
early infantile presentation is predominantly visceral, with hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice, and
pulmonary disease, although some also have neurological symptoms (Seker Yilmaz et al. 2020).
Individuals with early infantile onset tend to have a rapidly progressive disease course,
progressing to death in early childhood. The late infantile and juvenile presentations are
predominantly neurological with progressive decline in previously attained cognitive, gross, and
fine motor skills as well as progressive dysarthria and dysphagia. Additional neurological
symptoms include ataxia, cataplexy, vertical supranuclear gaze palsy, and epilepsy. Adolescent
and adult onset of disease can be more insidious, with cognitive and behavioral changes or other
psychiatric manifestations at the onset, followed by chronic neurological decline (Las Heras et
al. 2023).
Disease progression most commonly impacts ambulation, fine motor skills, speech, swallowing,
and cognition, ultimately progressing to respiratory failure and death. Progressive neurological
symptoms also include ataxia and other movement disorder symptoms and epilepsy. Ataxia is the
most common movement disorder that occurs in NPC and can sometimes be a presenting
symptom of the disease (Devaraj et al. 2022) (Anheim et al. 2014). While lifespan can vary from
a few days (severe neonatal onset) to a few decades, the reported median age of death is 13 years
(range: 0.1 to 69 years) (Bianconi et al. 2019).
10
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
An Externally Lead Patient-Focused Drug Development meeting was held on March 18, 2019
and was hosted by 8 patient advocacy organizations for NPC.1 During the meeting, patients and
caregivers shared their perspectives on disease symptoms, the impact of NPC on their daily lives,
current experience with treatment, and expectations for future treatments. Eighty-three patients
and caregivers responded to a survey prior to the meeting; the age of onset of neurological
symptoms (developmental delays, ataxia, vertical gaze palsy, etc.) for most patients was after the
age of 2 years (36.7% were 2 to 5 years old, 27.8% were 6 to 15 years old, and 12.7% were 16 to
29 years old). The six symptoms reported as having the greatest impact on patient and
caregivers’ daily lives were ambulation/walking difficulties and cognitive impairment, followed
by swallowing difficulty, speech problems, seizures, and fine motor skills. Ambulation/walking
and fine motor skills are often significantly impacted by ataxia in NPC; for example, one father
of a 16-year-old male who had the onset of impaired coordination at 8 years old said “Less than
one year after ataxia first appeared, his condition had deteriorated markedly. His gait became
staggered, his handwriting reverted to kindergarten level, and the ataxia became more
pronounced. Simple activities such as brushing teeth, or shampooing hair, became difficult
tasks.” The most meaningful targets for drug treatment were summarized as impacting
neurological disease symptoms, including any slowing, halting, or improvement of disease
symptoms.
In the United States, there is only one recently approved treatment for the neurological
manifestations of NPC; additional therapeutic options for this devastating disease are needed.
The current standard of care is primarily supportive and includes the treatment of epilepsy with
anti-seizure medications as well as interventions to support feeding, mobility, communication,
and behavior. Miglustat is an iminosugar that acts as a competitive and reversible inhibitor of the
enzyme glucosylceramide synthase, the initial enzyme in the synthesis of many
glycosphingolipids (GSLs). Miglustat was approved in the United States in 2003 for patients
with mild or moderate Gaucher disease when enzyme replacement therapy is not a therapeutic
option. Miglustat was also approved for the treatment of the progressive neurological
manifestations of NPC in pediatric and adult patients in many countries outside the United
States. Miglustat is often prescribed “off-label” for patients with NPC in the United States and is
considered standard of care for NPC amongst clinical experts.
Patients with NPC have a substantial and unmet treatment need. While miglustat has been
reported in the medical literature to potentially slow disease progression in some patients, NPC
remains progressive and life-limiting in all affected individuals. In the Voice of the Patient report
for NPC (Externally Lead Patient-Focused Drug Development meeting) (EL-PFDD 2019), this
unmet need was summarized as follows: “Patients and families most desire new treatments for
neurological symptoms and improved quality of life even if they do not extend life span.
Recognizing the heterogeneity of disease progression and symptoms, patients with NPC and
their families emphasize the need for multiple therapeutic options so that treatment approaches
can be tailored to meet individual patient needs.”
N-acetyl-L-leucine (levacetylleucine) is a modified amino acid and is the L-enantiomer of
N-acetyl-DL-leucine. The racemate N-acetyl-DL-leucine has been available in France since 1957
1 Externally Lead Patient-Focused Drug Development Meeting on March 18, 2019 was hosted by: Ara Parseghian

Medical Research Fund at Notre Dame, Hide & Seek Foundation, Dana’s Angels Research Trust, Hope for Marian,
National Niemann-Pick Disease Foundation, Niemann-Pick Canada, Firefly Fund, and Johnathon’s Dreams.

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
as a short-term oral treatment in tablet form primarily used for acute vertigo in adults. The
Applicant states that levacetylleucine reduced unesterified cholesterol in fibroblasts derived from
patients with NPC. In addition, the Applicant cited published data in which Npc1-/- mice treated
with levacetylleucine showed a slight reduction in a subset of GSLs in the brain (GM2, GM1a)
and a small reduction in GA1 and GA2 in the cerebellum (Kaya et al. 2021). The magnitudes of
these effects were sufficiently small that it is unclear if they were physiologically meaningful;
however, there was an apparent reduction in CD68 staining in Npc1-/- mice that were treated with
levacetylleucine compared with that of untreated Npc1-/- mice. These changes were accompanied
by histological evidence of reduced CD68 in the cerebellum in levacetylleucine-treated animals
compared with untreated Npc1-/- mice. In addition, some markers of motor improvement were
observed; however, the magnitude of the effect was relatively small. These effects were
accompanied by a small (8-day) increase in median survival.

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
The Applicant submitted data from two trials that enrolled patients with NPC and one trial that
enrolled patients with a different lysosomal storage disorder, GM2 gangliosidosis. The benefitrisk assessment is based on the single adequate and well-controlled trial in subjects with NPC
(Trial IB1001-301) with supportive evidence from an open-label rater-blinded study (Trial
IB1001-201) in subjects with NPC. The trial in GM2 gangliosidosis was reviewed for safety
signals and trial withdrawals or deaths only and was not included in our efficacy assessment.
The clinical trials conducted to support the benefit-risk assessment of levacetylleucine for the
treatment of the neurological manifestations of NPC are summarized in Table 3.
Determination of Efficacy
To evaluate the efficacy of levacetylleucine for treating the neurological symptoms of NPC, the
results of the randomized, double-blinded period of phase 3 clinical trial IB1001-301 was
considered the primary source of efficacy data. A total of 60 subjects who received both
levacetylleucine and placebo in Trial IB1001-301 were evaluated. At the time of NDA
submission on January 24, 2024, an open-label extension (OLE) phase was ongoing, and the
efficacy data were not submitted. In addition to Trial IB1001-301, the open-label phase 2 clinical
trial IB1001-201 provided confirmatory evidence (CE).
Determination of Safety
The safety assessment was primarily based on data from Trial IB1001-301, the only source of
randomized, placebo-controlled data in subjects with NPC. The safety data from the open-label
study of levacetylleucine in subjects with NPC, Trial IB1001-201, as well as available data from
the OLEs of both Trials IB1001-301 and IB1001-201 were also reviewed separately. The NPC
safety population consisted of 84 subjects who received levacetylleucine, including 28 pediatric
subjects and 56 adult subjects. Lastly, safety data from Trial IB1001-202, an open-label study of
levacetylleucine in GM2 gangliosidosis, was also reviewed for potential safety signals.
The analysis of general safety assessment of adverse events (AEs), laboratory evaluations, vital
signs, and electrocardiograms was based on descriptive summaries and review of source data.
Case report forms and subject narratives were also reviewed for premature trial discontinuations
or withdrawals. Clinical trial data were independently analyzed using JMP and Python software.
All safety assessments and conclusions are those of the clinical review team unless otherwise
specified.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
NPC is a lysosomal storage disease that is biochemically characterized by increased storage of
multiple lipids including low-density lipoprotein -derived cholesterol, sphingoid bases (e.g.,
sphingosine and sphinganine), and other sphingolipids including GSLs and sphingomyelin. The
combined biochemical and cellular defects in NPC lead to widespread pathology in the brain
including neurodegeneration and neuroinflammation, and to the clinical signs of NPC. The
pharmacological effects of levacetylleucine (also referred to as N-acetyl-L-leucine, NALL, or
IB1001) in treating NPC are discussed here. It is concluded that the pharmacology parameters
evaluated in nonclinical models are primarily proof-of-concept that suggest plausible
mechanistic support for the overall package of CE of effectiveness for levacetylleucine in
patients with NPC.
In their submission, the Applicant cited published data that describe potential modes of action
whereby treatment with levacetylleucine slows the decline of biochemical and cellular defects
present in nonclinical models of NPC, both in vitro and in vivo. These publications suggest that
treatment with levacetylleucine may reduce accumulation of sphingosine, GSLs, and cholesterol,
and that this treatment reduced lysosomal volume in NPC1-deficient Chinese hamster ovary cells
in vitro. In vitro levacetylleucine treatment was also found to reduce lysosomal volume in
cultured fibroblasts obtained from patients with NPC.
In vivo studies in Npc1-/- mice, a nonclinical model of NPC, were conducted in which animals
were treated pre-symptomatically (i.e., from the age of 3 weeks). The authors reported a
reduction in total GSL levels in the forebrain (GM1a and GM2) and cerebellum (GA2 and GA1).
The authors also reported a reduction in ataxic symptoms in Npc1-/- mice treated presymptomatically at a later age (8 weeks old). When initiated at the age of 3 weeks, treatment
with levacetylleucine was also associated with a modest increase in survival and a reduction in
markers of neuroinflammation (CD68 staining) in the cerebellum.
These findings suggest that levacetylleucine may slow disease progression in mice by an
undefined mechanism; however, these findings do not provide direct support for the primary
efficacy endpoint measured in clinical trials (Trial IB1001-201, Trial IB1001-301, or both). As
such, the nonclinical data can only suggest plausible mechanistic CE for levacetylleucine in
treating patients with NPC and should only be considered as a contributing factor in conjunction
with strong evidence of clinical efficacy in the phase 3 clinical trial, Trial IB1001-301,
conducted in patients with NPC.
For additional details of cited publication see Section 13.2.2.

5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
The pharmacologic activity, pharmacokinetics (PK), and clinical pharmacology of levacetylleucine that are relevant to the
interpretation of benefit and risk are summarized in Table 5.
Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics
Characteristic
Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Mechanism of action
The mechanism of action of levacetylleucine is unknown.
Active moieties
N-acetyl-L-leucine (NALL) is the active moiety. After being absorbed, NALL is metabolized into acetate and L-leucine
by ubiquitously expressed enzymes. No other active metabolite of NALL has been identified.
QT prolongation
The Applicant has not conducted a thorough QT/QTc study to assess the effect of NALL on QT interval prolongation.
According to the applicant’s evaluation, the nonclinical and clinical data demonstrated no potential for QT/QTc
prolongation and no other cardiovascular related findings. The review team agreed with the applicant’s evaluation
and agreed with waiving a TQT study. See Cardiac IRT team review in DARRTS dated January 26, 2024 (Reference
ID: 5317889)
General Information
Bioanalysis
Levacetylleucine concentrations in human plasma were quantified using a validated LC-MS/MS assay. The
sensitivity of the assay was 100 ng/mL.

Drug Information
Absorption
The absolute bioavailability of NALL following oral administration has not been determined.
After oral administration, NALL was absorbed with median (min, max) Tmax of 1 (0.5, 2.5) hour.
Distribution
The mean (SD) volume of distribution at steady state (Vss) was 253 (125) L.
Plasma protein binding of NALL is very low with free drug about 100%.
Elimination
The mean (SD) clearance is 139 (59) L/hour.
The estimated mean half-life is around 1 hour.
Levacetylleucine is metabolized into acetate and L-leucine by ubiquitously expressed enzymes, which are used
endogenously in catabolic and metabolic pathways. Cytochrome P450 enzymes are not involved in the metabolism
of levacetylleucine.
No mass balance study was conducted in humans. Following a single oral administration to rats, the metabolites are
the amino acid L-leucine and acetic acid.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
In the population PK analysis, body weight was identified as a significant covariate; apparent clearance (CL/F) and
apparent volume of distribution (V/F) of NALL were lower in subjects with lower body weight. The recommended
dosing regimen of NALL in patients with NPC is based on body weight.
Age was not identified as significant covariate affecting NALL PK in the population PK analyses.
The effect of renal impairment on the PK of IB1001 has not been evaluated. See Section 8.1.
The effect of hepatic impairment on the PK of IB1001 has not been evaluated. See Section 8.1.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
No in vitro induction towards CYP enzymes was observed. No clinical drug-drug interaction studies have been
conducted.
Inhibition of efflux transporters P-gp, BCRP, BSEP and OAT1/3 by NALL was observed in vitro.

6.1.3. Exposure-Response Relationship for Efficacy
In the E-R analysis conducted based on the data from 38 subjects who had both efficacy and
evaluable pharmacokinetic (PK) data in Trial IB1001-301, no E-R relationship was observed
between the functional scale for assessment and rating of ataxia (fSARA) and the exposure
metric, daily area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) at steady state at the proposed
dosing regimen (Figure 1). However, due to the limited sample size, the E-R relationship for
efficacy is inconclusive.

6.1.4. Exposure-Response Relationship for Safety
Due to the limited incident rate for each treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) (refer to

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
The Applicant’s clinical trials to investigate levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC consist of
two studies: a multicenter, open-label, single arm study, Trial IB1001-201, and a pivotal
placebo-controlled, randomized, crossover study, Trial IB1001-301. Trial IB1001-301 is
considered a single adequate and well-controlled trial and will be the primary source to establish
effectiveness.

6.2.1. Trial IB1001-201
6.2.1.1. Design
Parent Study
Figure 2. Parent Study Design, Trial IB1001-201
A. Naïve Subjects

B. Non-Naïve Subjects

Source: Trial IB1001-201 Clinical Study Report, page 3-4

26
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Trial IB1001-201 was a multinational, multicenter, open-label, single-arm phase 2 study. A total
of 34 participants were screened, of whom 33 (97.1%) were treated with levacetylleucine. One
subject was considered a screen failure due to violation of inclusion/exclusion criteria. The
parent study of Trial IB1001-201 consisted of a 2-week baseline period, a 6-week treatment
period, and a 6-week washout period. Two visits were planned for each period. Due to
coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID-19) restrictions, Visit 3 and Visit 5 were classified as nonessential visits and were allowed to be skipped, while Visit 4 and Visit 6 remained essential
visits.
At the initial screening visit, subjects were classified as either naïve or non-naïve based on the
use of prohibited medication within the previous 42 days. For naïve subjects, the initial screening
visit was treated as Baseline 1. For non-naïve subjects, the initial screening visit was treated as
Visit 0 and the subjects underwent a minimum of 42 days of washout before Visit 1 (Baseline 1).
During the treatment period, all subjects received 1000 mg powder for oral suspension in 60 mL
glass bottles for approximately 42 days (+7 days).
The primary efficacy endpoint of the parent study was based on the blinded raters’ clinical
impression of change in severity (CI-CS) score comparing paired videos of the subject’s
performance on the predefined anchor test of either the 9-Hole Peg Test of the dominant hand
(9HPT-D) or the 8-meter walk test (8MWT) at two visits. At Visit 1, the investigating physician
selected a primary anchor test for each subject based on the predefined criteria. See Section 16.1
for the criteria.
For the CI-CS assessment, two independent, primary raters were provided with 3 pairs of video
recordings and asked to assess for each video pair: ‘Compared to the first video, how has the
severity of their performance on the 9HPT-D or 8MWT changed (improved or worsened) in 6weeks as observed in the second video?’ The video pairs that were made from videos at Visit 2,
Visit 4, and Visit 6 were presented in random order, and the internal pairing of each video set
was randomized. The raters were blinded to the timepoint of videos. The two primary raters’
scores were averaged if there was a 1-point difference between the scores given by the two
raters. If a difference was greater than 1 point, a third blinded rater reviewed the assessments and
determined the most accurate assessment between the two scores.
The primary endpoint was the CI-CS comparing the end of treatment (Visit 4) with baseline
(Visit 2) minus the CI-CS comparing the end of washout (Visit 6) with the end of treatment
(Visit 4). Each CI-CS score is measured on a 7-point Likert scale (-3=significantly worse to
+3=significantly improved).
While the evaluation of the primary endpoint CI-CS and a secondary endpoint Clinical
Impression of Severity were performed by two blinded, independent neurologists based on
videos of the anchor test, other secondary endpoints were assessed without blinding.
See Section 16 for the secondary and exploratory endpoints of the parent study.

27
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Figure 3. Extension Phase

Source: Trial IB1001-201 Clinical Study Report, page 4
Abbreviations: EOS = end of study; EP = extension phase

Upon completion of the parent study, eligible subjects were offered to participate in the
extension phase (EP) that allows subjects to continue treatment with levacetylleucine for
approximately 2 years. The EP comprises 6 visits over a 116-week period. The first visit of the
EP (Visit 7) was conducted 1 day after Visit 6 at the end of the parent study. All subjects were
planned to receive levacetylleucine for 365 days of EP Treatment Period I, followed by 42 days
of a washout period. After the washout period, subjects were to receive levacetylleucine for
365 days of EP Treatment Period II.
All endpoints in the EP were considered secondary to those in the parent study. The primary
endpoint in this EP was based on the 5-domain Niemann-Pick disease type C clinical severity
scale (5DNPCCSS) from the EP baseline (Visit 7) to the end of treatment in the EP (Visit 9). The
5-domain NPCCSS is comprised of 5 major domains (ambulation, speech, swallow, fine motor
skills, cognition), which add up to a composite value.
6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria
Parent Study
Inclusion Criteria
Individuals who met all the following criteria were eligible to participate in Trial IB1001-201:
1. Written informed consent signed by the patient and/or their legal representative/
parent/impartial witness.
2. Male or female aged ≥6 years in Europe OR ≥18 years in the United States with a confirmed
diagnosis of NPC at the time of signing informed consent. For individuals in Europe
confirmed diagnosis included:
a. Clinical features and positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test without genetic test
results (had not been performed).
b. Clinical features and positive genetic test.
c. Clinical features and positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test but only one NPC
mutation identified on genetic test.
d. Clinical features with positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test and positive genetic
test.
3. Females of childbearing potential, defined as a premenopausal female capable of becoming
pregnant, were included if they were either sexually inactive (sexually abstinent for 14 days
prior to the first dose and confirmed to continue through 28 days after the last dose) or using
28

a. Intrauterine device

c. Combined (estrogen or progestogen containing) hormonal contraception associated with
the inhibition of ovulation (either oral, intravaginal, or transdermal)
d. Progestogen only hormonal contraception associated with the inhibition of ovulation

been in the postmenopausal range as determined by the central laboratory.
5. Non-vasectomized male patients agreed to use a condom with spermicide or abstain from
sexual intercourse during the study until 90 days beyond the last dose of study medication
and the female partner agreed to comply with inclusion criteria 3 or 4. For a vasectomized

use a condom during sexual intercourse. A male who had been vasectomized less than
6 months prior to study start had to follow the same restrictions as a non-vasectomized male.

dose.

a. A scale for the assessment and rating of ataxia (SARA) score of 5 ≤ X ≤ 33 points (out of
40).

b. Either:

10. Had an understanding of the implications of study participation, provided in the written
patient information and informed consent by patients or their legal representative/parent, and
demonstrated a willingness to comply with instructions and attend required study visits (for
children this criterion would also be assessed in parents or appointed guardians).

6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan

The statistical analysis plan (SAP) was finalized on January 26, 2023 (Version 4.0) before
database lock on April 5, 2023. The data cut-off date for efficacy analyses of the parent study
was June 22, 2022. For the parent study, analyses were planned in the SAP Version 2.0 dated

31
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
May 20, 2020 and its COVID-19 addendum Version 1.0 dated September 16, 2020. For the EP,
analyses were planned in SAP Version 4.0.
According to the SAP, the intent-to-treat (ITT) population consists of all subjects who receive at
least one dose of levacetylleucine and have a video recording at either Visit 1 or Visit 2, or both.
The modified intent-to-treat (mITT) population is defined as all subjects in the ITT population
with one video recording at either Visit 3 or Visit 4, or both. The Per Protocol Set consists of all
subjects with at least one video recording at baseline (Visit 1 or Visit 2), end of treatment (Visit
3 or Visit 4), and end of washout (Visit 5 or Visit 6), and without any major protocol deviation
that could have influenced the validity of the data for the primary efficacy variable.
The primary analysis population for the primary and all secondary efficacy endpoints was the
mITT population. A last observation carried forward (LOCF) approach was used within each
period if a video is missing or unreadable. Also, if either Visit 3 or Visit 4 was available but both
Visit 5 and Visit 6 were missing or unreadable, the CI-CS score was assigned the value 0 (stable)
for the primary efficacy analysis.
6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses
6.2.1.4.1. Subject Disposition and Baseline Characteristics
Parent Study
A total of 33 subjects were enrolled in the parent study of Trial IB1001-201 and treated across
9 sites in the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, Spain, and Slovakia. Table 8
presents subject disposition. Two subjects (6.1%) withdrew from the parent study. Of those two,
one subject was withdrawn after Visit 2 due to an AE (increased seizure [n=1; 3.0%]) and was
not included in the mITT population. This subject began to have mild seizures after 9 days of
treatment. Given their medical history of seizures, the AE was not considered related to study
drug. The other subject was withdrawn after Visit 3 due to “Other” reason (“Intake of study drug
did not lead to benefit” [n=1; 3.0%]).

6.2.1.4.2. Efficacy Results for CI-CS
The results of the Applicant’s analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint are summarized in Table
10. The analyses of CI-CS were based on the prespecified mITT population that includes all
subjects in the ITT population with one recorded video at either Visit 3 or Visit 4, or both. The
mITT set includes 28 subjects who have completed all essential visits of the parent study, 3
subjects who have completed Visit 4 (end of treatment period) but have Visit 6 excluded from
the analysis due to extremely delayed visits, and 1 early termination subject who was withdrawn
after Visit 3 due to an unrelated serious adverse event (SAE). Within the mITT population, the
9HPT-D was selected for 20 subjects (62.5%) and the 8MWT was selected for 12 subjects
(37.5%). The mean CI-CS score in the treatment period (Visit 4 versus Visit 2) was 0.5 (SD=1.3)
indicating improvement in the subjects’ condition at the end of the treatment period (EOT). The
mean CI-CS score in the washout period (Visit 6 versus Visit 4) was -0.4 (SD=1.4) indicating
deterioration in the subjects’ condition at the end of the washout period (EOW).
The Applicant provided results based on the Wilcoxon signed-rank test (one-sided p-value
=0.03) and the Hodges-Lehmann estimate (median of the difference =1, 90% confidence interval
(CI): 0.25, 1.75). The estimated difference in mean CI-CS between the two treatment periods
was 0.9 with the p-value of 0.03 based on the one-sample t-test.

and the 9HPT-D subgroup, respectively. The results indicate improvement in their primary
anchor test during the treatment period followed by deterioration during the washout period in
both subgroups.

6.2.1.4.3. Efficacy Results for 8-Meter Walk Test and 9Hole Peg Test of the Dominant Hand
In addition to evaluation of the subject’s performance regarding quality of walking and upper
extremity function using the rater-blinded CI-CS endpoint, the quantitative analyses based on
speed of gait and dexterity were assessed using the timed 8MWT and 9HPT-D. Both tests were
provided as subtests of a secondary endpoint, SCAFI, which was assessed at every visit in the
parent study except Visit 0 (screening visit). For each subtest, subjects performed two
consecutive trials (Trial 1 and Trial 2). Time was reported within 0.1 second. At every visit, Trial
2 of the 8MWT and 9HPT-D were videoed as the CI-CS primary anchor test.
According to the CI-CS administration and scoring manual version 3.0 dated January 21, 2020,
the 8MWT was still administered and video-recorded even if a subject required the use of
assistive devices, another person, or wall for support. Per the SAP, if a subject required the
unallowable support (another person or wall), the time for the trial was not recorded in the
SCAFI scoring but only in the CI-CS scoring.
Table 12 shows descriptive statistics of the timed 8MWT at the EOT and at the EOW. Per the
scoring manual, the subjects with assistive devices (n=5) or a remote visit due to COVID-19
restrictions (n=1) were counted as missing data in Table 12. For the ITT population, the mean
change from baseline in the 8MWT at the EOT was -1.1 seconds (SD=4.1, median=-0.3). The
mean change in the 8MWT at the EOW compared with the start of the washout period was -0.3
second (SD=2.8, median=0.3), indicating nearly no change during the washout period.
The subgroup of subjects whose primary anchor test was the 8MWT for the CI-CS endpoint
showed a greater improvement in the 8MWT during the treatment period compared with the
subgroup whose primary anchor test was 9HPT-D for the CI-CS endpoint. The former subgroup
showed a mean change of -3.5 seconds (SD=5.5), and the latter showed a mean change of
0.1 (SD=2.7) during the treatment period. Considering the mean baseline difference between the
subgroups (17.7 versus 8.8 seconds) and the extent of changes in the 8MWT within the 9HPT-D
subgroup, the 8MWT subgroup is considered to have shown more notable improvement, while
the other subgroup already had stable gait function.

6.2.2. Trial IB1001-301
6.2.2.1. Design
The clinical benefit of levacetylleucine in patients with NPC was primarily based on a
multinational, randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled, crossover phase 3 study,
Trial IB1001-301. This trial involves 11 trial sites across 7 non-US countries. The trial consists
of three periods: a baseline period, a 12-week first treatment period (“Treatment Period I”), and a
12-week second treatment period (“Treatment Period II”). At the screening visit, subjects were
classified as either “naïve” or “non-naïve” based on their use of prohibited medications within
the past 42 days. A total of 60 subjects aged ≥4 years with a confirmed diagnosis of NPC at the
time of signing the informed consent were enrolled and randomized in a 1:1 ratio to one of the
following treatment sequences:
•

Sequence 1 (N=30): levacetylleucine in Treatment Period I, followed by immediate crossover
to placebo in Treatment Period II.

•

Sequence 2 (N=30): placebo in Treatment Period I, followed by immediate crossover to
levacetylleucine in Treatment Period II.

Figure 4. Study Design, Naïve Subjects, Trial IB1001-301

Source: Trial IB1001-301 Clinical Study Report, page 31
All of 60 subjects were naïve subjects; they had not used prohibited medications within the past 42 days.

There was no washout period between the two periods. According to Section 2.5.3.1.2 of the
submitted Clinical Overview,2 the mean half-life of levacetylleucine is approximately 1 hour
with a peak concentration range values between 0.5 to 2.5 hours after single and repeated oral
dose. The Applicant stated that no or minor accumulation was observed after repeated
administration.
Upon the completion of the parent study, trial participants were offered the opportunity to
participate in a 2-year EP if the safety and tolerability during the parent study was considered
acceptable by the Investigator. The EP is ongoing, thus data from the EP were not submitted at
the time of the NDA submission (January 24, 2024).

2 Clinical Overview, received January 24, 2024.

40
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
The Applicant’s primary endpoint for the U.S. marketing application was a modified scale for
the assessment and rating of ataxia (mSARA) total score at the end of treatment period
(Visit 4 or Visit 6). The SARA is used to assess eight domains: gait, stance, sitting, speech
disturbance, finger chase, nose-finger test, fast alternating hand movements, and heel-shin slide.
The details on the scores for each domain are presented in Section 16.4. The SARA total score
ranges from 0 to 40, where 0 is the best neurological status and 40 is the worst. The mSARA
focuses on six domains: gait, speech disturbance, finger-chase test, nose-finger test, fast
alternating movements, and heel-shin test. The mSARA score ranges from 0 to 30. For the U.S.
marketing application, the original SARA total score was defined as a key secondary endpoint.
Review team’s comment: The Agency previously communicated to the Applicant that mSARA
was not considered clearly interpretable to convey clinical benefit in daily functioning of patients
with NPC. The Agency recommended an fSARA which focuses on four domains: gait, stance,
sitting, and speech disturbance. For these four domains, the Agency recommended a
modification to the scoring so that each domain has five possible scores: 0 (normal), 1, 2, 3, and
4 (worst severity); thus, the total fSARA score ranges from 0 to 16. Detailed regulatory history
and the Agency's recommended scores are available in Section 6.3.1. In this review, results
based on the fSARA will be considered as primary evidence of efficacy in patients with NPC.
6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria
Inclusion Criteria
Individuals who met all the following criteria were eligible to participate in Trial IB1001-301:
1. Written informed consent signed by the patient and/or their legal
representative/parent/impartial witness.
2. Male or female aged 4 years or older with a confirmed diagnosis of NPC at the time of
signing informed consent. Confirmed diagnosis includes one of the following:3
a. Clinical features and positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test without genetic test
results (has not been performed).
b. Clinical features and positive genetic test.
c. Clinical features and positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test but only 1 NPC
mutation identified on genetic test.
d. Clinical features with positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test and positive genetic
test.

3 Note: The U.S. protocol required a confirmed genetic diagnosis of NPC. In the end, all 60 patients enrolled in the

trial ultimately had a confirmed genetic diagnosis of NPC.

41
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
3. Females of childbearing potential, defined as a premenopausal female capable of becoming
pregnant, were included if they were either sexually inactive (sexually abstinent4 for 14 days
prior to the first dose and confirmed to continue through 28 days after the last dose) or using
one of the following highly effective contraceptives (i.e., resulting in <1% failure rate when
used consistently and correctly) 14 days prior to the first dose continuing through 28 days
after the last dose:
a. Intrauterine device
b. Surgical sterilization of the partner (vasectomy for 6 months minimum)
c. Combined (estrogen or progestogen containing) hormonal contraception associated with
the inhibition of ovulation (either oral, intravaginal, or transdermal)
d. Progestogen only hormonal contraception associated with the inhibition of ovulation
(either oral, injectable, or implantable)
e. Intrauterine hormone-releasing system
f. Bilateral tubal occlusion
4. Females of non-childbearing potential who had to have undergone one of the following
sterilization procedures at least 6 months prior to the first dose:
a. Hysteroscopic sterilization
b. Bilateral salpingectomy
c. Hysterectomy
d. Bilateral oophorectomy
OR be postmenopausal with amenorrhea for at least 1 year prior to the first dose and FSH
serum levels consistent with postmenopausal status. FSH analysis for postmenopausal
women was done at screening. FSH levels should have been in the postmenopausal range
as determined by the central laboratory.
5. Non-vasectomized male patients who agreed to use a condom with spermicide during the
trial until 90 days beyond the last dose of study medication and the female partner agreed to
comply with inclusion criteria 3 or 4. For a vasectomized male who had his vasectomy
6 months or more prior to study start, it was required that they use a condom during sexual
intercourse. A male who had been vasectomized less than 6 months prior to study start had to
follow the same restrictions as a non-vasectomized male.
6. If male, patient agreed not to donate sperm from the first dose until 90 days after their last
dose.

4 Sexual abstinence is considered a highly effective method only if defined as refraining from heterosexual

intercourse during the entire period of risk associated with the study treatments. In this trial, abstinence was only
acceptable if in line with the subject’s preferred and usual lifestyle.
Periodic abstinence (calendar, symptothermal, and postovulation methods), withdrawal (coitus interruptus),
spermicides only, and lactational amenorrhoea method were not acceptable methods of contraception. As well,
female condom and male condom were not to be used together.

flavor).

discretion of the Investigator, Medical Monitor, and Sponsor, the washout period for specific
IMPs may be longer based on the pharmacological activity and PK of the drug.
3. Patients with a physical or psychiatric condition which, at the Investigator’s discretion and in
consultation with the Medical Monitor and Sponsor (as applicable), could put the patient at
risk, confound the study results, or interfere with the patient’s participation in the clinical
trial, (i.e., reliably perform trial assessments).

6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan

The SAP was finalized on September 29, 2022 (Version 1.0), prior to database lock and
unblinding for the parent phase on June 14, 2023. The primary efficacy analysis population was
based on the ITT population that consists of all randomized subjects who received at least one
dose of IMP (levacetylleucine or placebo). The primary efficacy endpoint, mSARA at Visit 4 or
Visit 6, was analyzed using a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) with an unstructured
covariance structure. The MMRM included treatment, period, sequence, baseline mSARA value
at Visit 2, and baseline-by-period interaction as fixed effects.
Handling of Treatment Discontinuation and Use of Rescue Medications
Per the SAP, a treatment policy strategy was considered for the primary analysis for the
following intercurrent events 1) treatment discontinuation due to AEs, 2) taking the
rescue/prohibited medications sulfasalazine or rosuvastatin, or 3) taking of prohibited
medications N-acetyl-DL-leucine (Tanganil, racemate of D and L enantiomers) or
levacetylleucine as a chemical grade substance or supplied outside of Trial IB1001-301.
Therefore, all data collected was used regardless of whether the intercurrent events occurred or
not. No subjects had the second and third types of intercurrent events. For missing data, a LOCF
on a per-period basis was applied.

Demographic baseline characteristics are presented in Table 16. The mean age of subjects was
26.4 (SD=15.9) years, ranging from 5 to 67 years old. Overall, 38.3% of subjects were pediatric
patients. Most subjects were White (90%), reflecting the demographics of the trial site locations
(most trial sites were in Europe). NPC is a genetic disorder, and the pathogenesis and clinical
course are not expected to differ amongst different racial and/or ethnic groups. Because there are
international management guidelines for NPC (Geberhiwot et al. 2018), there is reasonable
similarity in care practices of the European and American subjects. While there were no FDAapproved treatments for NPC, miglustat is approved for NPC in the non-US countries that
participated in the trial and is considered standard of care for patients with NPC. In the United
States, despite not being approved for NPC, off-label use is common. Most subjects (85%) in the
trial used miglustat at baseline. Miglustat is one of the non-prohibited medications; subjects were
allowed to take miglustat during the trial as long as they maintained a stable dose.

Subjects randomized to the placebo-levacetylleucine treatment sequence had higher mean
baseline (Visit 2) mSARA, fSARA, SARA, and 5DNPCCSS total scores than those who were
randomized to the other sequence (Table 17). The 5DNPCCSS total score was a prespecified
primary endpoint for the EP of Trial IB1001-301 and has been used as outcome measures in
natural history literature for patients with NPC (Yanjanin et al. 2010) (Mengel et al. 2020)
(Mengel et al. 2021).
The efficacy endpoints were highly correlated at baseline. The estimated pairwise Spearman
correlations between fSARA, mSARA, and SARA total score at baseline were ≥0.91. The
estimated Spearman correlations between 5DNPCCSS total score and the total scores of SARAbased endpoints were 0.72 (fSARA), 0.74 (mSARA), and 0.76 (SARA) at baseline. Additional
correlation analyses between the endpoints using all available visits are shown in Section 16.2.

There was no washout period between the two treatment periods. To address concerns about the
carryover effect, the statistical review team assessed a sequence effect in the primary analysis
model. The test for the sequence effect implied that there is no significant carryover effect
(p-value: 0.6027). In addition, between-subject comparison was conducted based on the first
period data only. The estimated treatment difference in the first period data was -0.35 (95% CI:
-0.90, 0.19) with a p-value of 0.202 using a two-sample t-test adjusting for baseline. While the
between-subject test is not adequately powered, the estimated treatment difference still indicates
improvement in fSARA total score with levacetylleucine compared to placebo.
The review team assessed the robustness of the primary efficacy results based on different

before trial discontinuation across all domains in both rescored and original scale. Since

Subgroup Analysis
Figure 7 shows subgroup analyses based on the fSARA. Overall, the subgroup analyses showed
a consistent trend of treatment difference, including for the subgroup of subjects not on
background miglustat. For the subgroup of seven subjects with baseline age under 10 years,
six subjects did not show treatment difference, while one subject experienced 1-unit
improvement when treated with levacetylleucine. Similarly, among the nine subjects who did not
take miglustat at baseline, eight subjects did not show treatment difference while one subject
experienced 1-unit improvement with levacetylleucine.
In addition to demographic subgroup analyses, the statistical review team conducted domainspecific analyses for gait, stance, sitting, and speech disturbance domains. The observed
treatment differences between levacetylleucine and placebo for rescored domains fSARA were 0.07 (95% CI: -0.17, 0.03) for gait, -0.13 (95% CI: -0.24, -0.03) for stance, -0.13 (95% CI: -0.25,
-0.01) for sitting, and -0.13 (95% CI: -0.28, 0.01) for speech disturbance. While the observed
difference was minimal in gait, the review team could not find any item that mainly drives the

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Interpretability of the Primary Efficacy Endpoint
Issue
There are two issues that lead to uncertainty regarding whether the observed changes in the
primary efficacy endpoint reflect a clinically meaningful benefit to patients with NPC. These
issues include scoring and interpretability of the SARA and the assessment of clinically
meaningful improvement in neurological symptoms, which are discussed below.
6.3.1.1. SARA Domain Selection and Rescoring
The Applicant and FDA differed on which SARA domains reflected pragmatic, noticeable
changes that communicated clinical benefit suitable for inclusion in a primary efficacy endpoint
(b) (4)

Background
The Agency recommended revising the response categories of the SARA so that the domain
response options are distinct and clearly interpretable. The Agency recommended using only the
four SARA domains of gait, stance, sitting, and speech (i.e., SARA domains #1, #2, #3, and #4,
respectively; advice letter dated December 20, 2021; advice letter dated March 21, 2022). The
Applicant defined the primary efficacy endpoint in Trial IB1001-301 based on the mSARA,
which comprises 6 or the 8 domains evaluated by the SARA: gait (domain 1), speech disturbance
(domain 4), finger chase (domain 5), nose-finger test (domain 6), fast alternating hand movement
(domain 7), and heel-shin slide (domain 8). The Applicant stated that NPC clinical experts and
patients with NPC/their caregivers indicated that fine motor, ambulation, and speech were the
top NPC outcomes to measure, that the selected 6 domains accounted for 90% of the
improvements observed in the phase 2 study, and that NPC experts indicated changes in SARA
domains 5 through 8 were considered to be associated with functional activities for patients with
NPC. Postural balance (sitting, stance) was not accounted for among responses, reflecting expert
consensus that it is not a key outcome. Scoring of the SARA domains varies (e.g., gait comprises
9 levels, speech comprises 7 levels), which interferes with the interpretation of the total score.
Assessment
Consistent with Agency’s approach in other rare progressive neurodegenerative indications
(Potashman et al. 2024b), several domains of the SARA (finger chase, nose to finger, fast
alternating hand movements, heel to shin slide) assess neurologic exam findings. Evidence
demonstrating that a change in SARA domains 5 through 8 was directly associated with
functional activities for patients was not available for review. These neurologic exam findings do
not directly assess the patient’s ability to function, nor do they reflect clinically meaningful
change in a patient’s ability to perform daily functions, making them unsuitable for inclusion in a
primary efficacy endpoint to convey clinical benefit. The Agency acknowledges that postural
control is not reported as an important outcome in NPC (Parseghian 2019); however, the
measurement of sitting and stance are functional abilities where a change in scores clearly
58
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
correspond to daily functioning. As the Applicant did not revise the response categories of the
SARA domains, the Agency rescored to create the same number of response categories across
each domain (Table 23) to ensure equal representation of each domain in the total score and
interpretability of a 1-point change as clinically meaningful (Potashman et al. 2024a).
Conclusion
The Agency accepts the fSARA domains of gait (domain 1), stance (domain 2), sitting (domain
3), and speech (domain 4) with rescoring so that all domains have equal response categories.
6.3.1.2. Interpretability of the SARA in Young Children
Ataxia and related domains are difficult to measure in young children with a progressive
condition, a measurement challenge that is not specific to the SARA. SARA scores can have low
reliability in developing young children with progressive conditions, which may interfere with
interpretability (Brandsma et al. 2014) (Lawerman et al. 2017). The Applicant indicated interchild variation in SARA ratings may be mitigated by the cross-over study design; however, the
small population of subjects <10 years old made interpretation challenging.
Conclusion
Establishing efficacy in pediatric subjects <10 years old will rely on extrapolation from subjects
≥10 years old.

determined the fSARA response options represented distinct and meaningfully different health

The Agency conducted responder analyses and defined a responder as a subject who had at least
1-point improvement from baseline in any of the four fSARA domains (i.e., gait, stance, sitting,
and speech). The responder analysis was performed for subjects who completed both Visit 4 and
(b) (6)
Visit 6. Consequently, Subjects
were excluded from the analysis. For
brevity, the review team will interchange the term ‘treatment period’ and ‘period’. There were
33 treatment responders (56.8%) in the trial: 13 subjects (22.4%) responded in the
levacetylleucine period and not in the placebo period, 2 subjects (3.4%) responded in the placebo
period and not in the levacetylleucine period, and 18 subjects responded in both periods (31%).
The estimated difference in response rates between the subjects who responded in only one of
the treatment periods was 18.9% (95% CI using 10,000 resampling datasets: 6.9%, 31.0%).
Among subjects who responded in at least one period during the trial, there were a total of
31 responders (53.4%) in the levacetylleucine period, and 20 responders (34.5%) in the placebo
period (Table 24).
To test nonequality of treatment effects between levacetylleucine and placebo in the
dichotomous crossover data, the Mainland-Gart test was conducted. Since the number of subjects
who contribute to the contingency table is small, we used a Fisher’s exact version of the
Mainland-Gart test to evaluate non-equality of treatment effects. The p-value of 0.011 suggests
that there is strong evidence that the effects on subjects having at least 1-point improvement in
any domain are not equal between placebo and levacetylleucine, favoring levacetylleucine.

Additionally, given the heterogeneity of NPC, it is important to evaluate individual changes in
the total fSARA and in each of its four domains. For that purpose, dumbbell plots of change
from baseline to EOT (i.e., Visit 4 or Visit 6) in fSARA (Figure 8) and each of the four domain
scores (Figure 32 to Figure 35 in Section 16.3) were visualized. The dumbbell plot (Figure 8)
shows the change from baseline to end of treatment in fSARA during the levacetylleucine period
(green circle) and placebo period (purple diamond) for each subject. During the levacetylleucine
period, 72.9% of subjects experienced no change in fSARA from baseline compared to 72.4% of
subjects during the placebo period. For those who experienced changes, more subjects
experienced improvement while in the levacetylleucine period (20.3%) as compared to when
they were in the placebo period (15.5%), whereas more subjects experienced worsening while in
the placebo period (12.1%) than while in the levacetylleucine period (6.8%). Subjects in general
experienced more improvement in the fSARA when they received levacetylleucine as compared
to when they received placebo (i.e., the green open circle is on the left of the purple diamond),
with few exceptions (e.g., 4 [6.9%] subjects improved more in the fSARA on placebo than when
they were in the levacetylleucine period).

Overall, similar trends of change described above were observed across the four domains. See
Section 16.3 for a detailed discussion.
The Applicant conducted exploratory exit surveys at the conclusion of Trial IB1001-301
(i.e., Visit 6 before unblinding) to provide additional evidence of clinical meaningfulness and
subject experiences with levacetylleucine. Subjects and caregivers provided reports of
improvements in neurological symptoms (e.g., swallowing, reduction in falls, cataplexy,
cognition). The exploratory evidence from the exit surveys may be viewed as supportive for the
broad treatment effects related to neurological symptoms. From a clinical perspective, NPC is a
progressive, neurodegenerative disease where neurological symptom stabilization and/or
improvement of any magnitude for any neurological symptom would not be expected given the
known natural history of the disease. Patients, caregivers and clinical experts in NPC have

64
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
indicated that slowing of disease progression as well as improvement in neurological symptoms
would be meaningful (Parseghian 2019).
Conclusion
While the observed average treatment effect in fSARA is -0.45, at least a 1-point improvement in
any of the four fSARA domains were seen more often when subjects received levacetylleucine
than received placebo. As improvements in neurological symptoms would not be expected given
the known natural history of NPC, this change is considered to be clinically meaningful.

6.3.2. Establishment of Drug Effectiveness in Patients
<10 Years Old
6.3.2.1. Body Weight ≥15 kg
Issue/Background
Because of the uncertainty regarding the interpretation of the primary efficacy endpoint in
subjects <10 years old (body weight ≥15 kg) and the very limited number of studied subjects in
this age group, there was a concern regarding the adequacy of the data from Trial IB1001-301 to
establish efficacy patients with NPC who are <10 years old.
Assessment
Of the 60 randomized subjects in Trial IB1001-301, 7 subjects (11.7%) were <10 years old.
According to a subgroup analysis, the observed treatment difference for this subpopulation was
-0.14 (95% CI: -0.49, 0.21; Figure 7). Among the 7 subjects <10 years old, only one subject
showed a treatment difference between IB1001 and placebo in fSARA score at the end of
treatment periods (Figure 9). Due to the small number of subjects in this subgroup, the review
team determined that the efficacy data from Trial IB1001-301 were not sufficient to demonstrate
a treatment benefit in subjects <10 years old.

65
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
(≥10 years old) to younger pediatric subjects (<10 years old). Of note, the youngest pediatric
subject included in the analysis was 6 years old.

Abbreviations: AUC = area under the concentration-time curve; Cmax = maximum plasma concentration; n = number of subjects with
given characteristic; SD = standard deviation

Conclusion
The establishment of efficacy in pediatric patients <10 years old relies on extrapolation of
efficacy seen in subjects ≥10 years old in Trial IB1001-301. The review team determined that
extrapolation of efficacy from subjects ≥10 years old to patients <10 years old is scientifically
justified based on the same underlying disease pathophysiology, similarity in clinical
manifestations, and the expected same therapeutic mechanism of action (MOA) across age
groups. The PK findings from Trials IB1001-201, IB1001-202, and IB1001-301 support this
extrapolation, as they provide evidence that the evaluated dose in subjects <10 years old results
in similar exposure to subjects ≥10 years old. Therefore, the efficacy results from Trial IB1001301 and PK data from Trials IB1001-201, IB1001-202, and IB1001-301 support the
effectiveness of IB1001 in patients <10 years old and support a labeled indication for pediatric
patients weighing ≥15 kg (refer to subsection below for a discussion on why the available data
do not support dosing in patients <15 kg).
