6.3.3. Adequacy of Confirmatory Evidence
Issue/Background
SEE, the regulatory requirement for approval (under 21 CFR 314.126), generally consists of
evidence from at least two adequate and well-controlled trials. In certain circumstances, a single
adequate and well-controlled trial demonstrating efficacy may be sufficient to generate SEE if
there is adequate CE. Such circumstances are described in the FDA draft guidance for industry
Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biological Products
(December 2019), which includes: the persuasiveness of the single investigation; the robustness
of the CE; the seriousness of the disease, particularly when there is an unmet medical need; the
size of the patient population; and whether it is ethical and practicable to conduct more than one
adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation. These criteria and considerations are all
relevant to the levacetylleucine development program for NPC, a rare and serious disease with
an unmet need. Because the levacetylleucine NDA includes only one adequate and wellcontrolled trial, the review team sought to assess whether there is adequate CE to generate SEE.
As stated in the FDA draft guidance for industry Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness With One Adequate and Well-Controlled Clinical Investigation and Confirmatory
Evidence (September 2023), the strength of the single adequate and well-controlled clinical trial
informs the quantity and quality of CE needed to substantiate the results of the trial. The highly
persuasive results of the single trial, Trial IB1001-301 (described in Section 6.2.2.4.3),
influenced the review team’s determination of the level of strength needed for the data to serve
as CE in this application.
The Applicant submitted data from multiple sources to serve as potential CE of the treatment
effect of levacetylleucine observed in Trial IB1001-301. These sources, which included
nonclinical studies (mechanistic in vitro and animal disease model) and clinical trials (a phase 2
single arm trial in patients with NPC and a phase 2 single arm trial in patients with GM2
gangliosidosis), are discussed in the Assessment section below.
Assessment
Nonclinical Evidence: Mechanistic Studies (In Vitro)
Data to support the MOA for levacetylleucine were derived from published literature. The
Applicant described data suggesting that in cultured Chinese hamster ovary cells, treatment with
levacetylleucine at a concentration of 1mM for 24 hours, was associated with a modest reduction
in lysosomal volume, sphingosine, GSLs, and a very modest reduction in stored cholesterol. In
NPC patient-derived fibroblasts, treatment with levacetylleucine was also associated with a
modest reduction in apparent lysosomal volume when cells were cultured for 7 days with 1mM
levacetylleucine. These studies suggest that the drug has the potential to reduce stored
intracellular lipid; however, the MOA, including any molecular targets by which it may elicit
these effects, is unclear.
Nonclinical Evidence (In Vivo)
In Npc1-/- mice, a nonclinical mouse model of NPC, pre-symptomatic (from the age of 3 weeks)
treatment with levacetylleucine was also associated with a modest increase in survival and a
reduction in markers of neuroinflammation (CD68 staining) in the cerebellum (see
70
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Section 13.2.2). These changes were not monitored in the clinical trial to evaluate the association
of change in magnitude of GSL and neuroinflammation with the dose of levacetylleucine and
efficacy. Taken together, the nonclinical data support a potential mechanism by which
levacetylleucine may reduce intracellular cholesterol and may improve survival when
administered pre-symptomatically in mice; however, because the same parameters were not
assessed in clinical trials, there is no direct correlation between these data and the efficacy
endpoints measured in subjects.
Additional Clinical Data
The additional clinical data submitted by the Applicant as potential sources of clinical CE
include the open-label study of levacetylleucine in patients with NPC (Trial IB1001-201), an
open-label study of levacetylleucine in individuals with GM2 gangliosidosis (Trial IB1001-202),
and several case series detailing off-label use of Tanganil (N-acetyl-DL-leucine) in NPC and
GM2 gangliosidosis.
Trial IB1001-201
The results from Trial IB1001-201 are based on the rater-blinded CI-CS in the parent study.
Thirty-three subjects with NPC were enrolled in the trial, which consisted of a 2-week baseline
period, a 6-week treatment period, and a 6-week washout period. The CI-CS measured each
subject’s change in performance on their primary anchor test (8MWT or 9HPT-D) between the
beginning and end of the treatment and washout periods. Improvements were observed in the
treatment period for the selected anchor test with the mean CI-CS score of 0.5 (median=1), but
performance declined by the EOW with the mean CI-CS score of -0.4 (median=-0.2). The
Hodges-Lehmann estimate for the median difference of CI-CS score between the two periods
was 1.0 (90% CI: 0.25, 1.75), indicating improvement in the treatment period and deterioration
during the washout period. The associated Wilcoxon signed rank test resulted in a one-sided pvalue of 0.03. The rater-blinded CI-CS endpoint relied on an investigator-selected primary
anchor test. Each anchor test was performed by all subjects as a component of the SCAFI (a
secondary endpoint). The timed SCAFI subtests showed minimal changes in 8MWT in both
periods, with mean changes of -1.1 and -0.3 seconds for the treatment and washout periods,
respectively, which were considered within measurement error. The 9HPT-D showed minor
changes in the treatment period with a mean change of 1.1 seconds, but a deterioration with a
mean change of 7.7 seconds after the washout.
While Trial IB1001-201 was an open-label study and therefore was susceptible to bias that
decreases the persuasiveness of these results, the methodology of blinding the CI-CS raters to
time and treatment assignment increases the interpretability of the resulting scores by reducing
the influence of knowledge of treatment assignment.
The CI-CS results supported by the timed SCAFI subtests indicate either improvement or
stability in the treatment period, and either deterioration or stability in the washout period.
Consequently, the Agency concludes that Trial IB1001-201 provided sufficient supportive
evidence that the subjects’ performance on the selected measures of gait and fine motor function
is comparatively favorable in the treatment period. Refer to Section 6.2.1 for detailed results.

71
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
treatment benefit on the primary endpoint (p-value: <0.001). The review team determined that, in
the context of these highly persuasive results from the pivotal trial, the strength of the submitted
CE – specifically, the results of Trial IB1001-201 and the supportive mechanistic nonclinical
data – was adequate to substantiate the results of Trial IB1001-301. Together, Trial IB1001-301
and the CE establish SEE of levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC, a rare disease with an
unmet therapeutic need. The data from both the adequate and well-controlled trial (Trial IB1001301) and the CE are adequate to support use of levacetylleucine either with or without
background concomitant miglustat.

6.3.4. Uncertainty Regarding Durability of Treatment
Effect
Issue/Background
The single adequate and well-controlled trial, Trial IB1001-301, assessed the efficacy of
levacetylleucine for NPC over a treatment period of 12 weeks. Because treatment with
levacetylleucine beyond 12 weeks occurred only during the OLE periods of Trial IB1001-301
and IB1001-201, the interpretability of the efficacy data to support long-term use was limited.
Assessment
For the pivotal Trial IB1001-301, the Applicant initially proposed a placebo-controlled trial
duration of 6 weeks based on preliminary findings from their open-label study, Trial IB1001201. During a Type C meeting held on May 27, 2001, a minimum placebo-controlled duration of
12 weeks for Trial IB1001-301 was advised to the Applicant by the FDA. Trial IB1001-301
incorporated 12 weeks of placebo-controlled treatment as agreed upon, followed by an OLE
study that is ongoing. Subjects who completed the trial were eligible to continue open-label
treatment with levacetylleucine for up to 2 additional years. After a midcycle meeting with the
Applicant in May 2024 during the review of this NDA, the Applicant provided a high-level
summary of data for the first year of the extension study. The primary endpoint for the EP is not
the same as for the parent study, thus a comparison of trial outcomes from 12 weeks of placebocontrolled treatment to the first year of open-label treatment would be challenging due to the
different timing of assessments and prespecified endpoints. The endpoint for the EP is the
change in 5DNPCCSS from baseline to the EOT.
The Applicant reported that 54 subjects enrolled in the extension study after completion of
Trial IB1001-301 and that all have completed the first 6 months of open-label treatment (Visit 8).
After 12 months from randomization baseline, the mean change on the 5DNPCCSS was
-0.115 (±2.60). The Applicant compared the mean change to that of published cohorts, however
given the lack of individual subject data for either the EP or the cohorts used for comparison, we
did not consider this comparison in our assessment. Generally, NPC is expected to progress after
the onset of neurological symptoms, thus a mean change that suggests decrease (rather than a
mean increase, which would suggest worsening) of 5DNPCCSS scores may indicates potential
disease stabilization and/or improvement in a portion of trial subjects, but this interpretation is
limited by the absence of a control group.
The Applicant also provided summary data from their version of the mSARA, the total SARA,
(b) (4)
and the fSARA,

Conclusion
Trial IB1001-301 provides evidence of a treatment benefit for the neurological symptoms of
NPC. The SARA scale from which the primary endpoint was constructed was developed to
(b) (4)
measure the symptoms of cerebellar ataxia specifically. However, in the context of
(b) (4)
different aspects of
pragmatic neurological function measured by the fSARA specifically, the data are adequate to
(b) (4)
support an indication that addresses the neurological manifestations of NPC
(b) (4)
It is not known what impact (if any) levacetylleucine has on the non-neurological
symptoms of NPC.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
•

There were no toxicologically significant findings in the 26-week rat and 39-week dog oral
gavage toxicology studies conducted with levacetylleucine. Safety margins achieved at the
no-observed-adverse-effect levels were 2- to 3-fold in rats and 6- to 9-fold in dogs compared
to the steady-state AUCs in humans at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD).

•

Embryofetal development toxicity, including malformations and embryofetal death, was
observed based on dose-ranging embryofetal developmental toxicology studies that were
conducted in rats and rabbits at 1.4- and 6-fold the MRHD based on body surface area.

•

Juvenile animal studies have not been conducted; therefore, it is unknown whether
levacetylleucine may affect developmental processes in children.

•

Nonclinical fertility studies have not been conducted; therefore, risks to male and female
fertility and early embryonic development are unknown.

•

Nonclinical pre- and postnatal development toxicity studies have not been conducted;
therefore, risks to pre- and postnatal development are unknown.

•

Rodent carcinogenicity studies have not been performed; therefore, carcinogenic risks are
unknown.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Not applicable. There are no other drugs in the same class as levacetylleucine.

76
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Not applicable. This product has not been approved in the Unites States or in any foreign
markets. Therefore, no postmarketing experience is available at the time of this review.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
Safety data from the trials listed in Table 3 were submitted to support the safety of
levacetylleucine. The safety review was primarily based on Trial IB1001-301, the only placebocontrolled study of levacetylleucine in subjects with NPC. Safety results from the open-label,
rater-blinded Trial IB1001-201 and the OLEs of both Trials IB1001-301 and IB1001-201 were
reviewed separately to evaluate any potential safety signals not identified in the pivotal trial or
that occurred after a longer duration of exposure to levacetylleucine. Lastly, summary data from
the completed open-label study of levacetylleucine in GM2 gangliosidosis (Trial IB1001-202)
was reviewed for additional safety signals, withdrawals, or deaths. An ongoing open-label study
of levacetylleucine in Ataxia-Telangiectasia (Trial IB1001-203) was not included because no
summary data were provided by the Applicant.
Because there are no clinical safety data for levacetylleucine in subjects under 5 years old, the
Agency requested any available real-world data from the literature or from treating clinicians
known to the Applicant. The Applicant did not have any available data for levacetylleucine but
did provide data supporting the safety of the racemate of levacetylleucine, Tanganil (N-acetylDL-leucine) in patients with NPC and GM2 gangliosidosis from a patient registry and a
clinician’s anecdotal reports. Medical literature including several case reports and case series
summarizing off-label use of Tanganil in NPC and other conditions that was included in the
NDA submission by the Applicant was not included in the safety evaluation because subjectlevel data for the age groups represented (which were primarily adults) was available for
levacetylleucine in the safety database.
The labelled adverse reactions (ARs) for Tanganil were reviewed as AEs that may overlap with
levacetylleucine (skin rashes, urticaria). However, the form of Tanganil marketed in France and
other countries uses a wheat-based excipient and is contraindicated in patients with a wheat
allergy; thus, it cannot be determined if ARs are related to the excipient rather than the active
ingredient. The majority (87%) of subjects in Trial IB1001-301 were taking concomitant
miglustat as part of their standard of care therapy and were on a stable dose at the time of
enrollment. Labelling for Zavesca (miglustat) for adults with Gaucher disease contains
‘Warnings and Precautions’ for the following: tremor, gastrointestinal events such as diarrhea
and weight loss, peripheral neuropathy, and thrombocytopenia. We considered the potential
impact of concomitant miglustat use on reported ARs with levacetylleucine exposure throughout
our review.

Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
The NPC safety population (including Trials IB1001-301 and IB1001-201) consisted of a total of
84 subjects who were between 5 to 67 years old. The lowest weight of a subject was 20.6 kg.
There were nine subjects who participated in both parent studies and were only counted once for
this total. Between the two trials, there were 28 pediatric subjects (7 were under 10 years old)
and 56 adult subjects.
First, the data from each trial with submitted datasets was examined for the required and
standardized components and for completeness of data. The review team then confirmed that the
AEs reported by the Applicant accurately described the AEs reported by the investigators. Once
accuracy was confirmed, the frequencies of each AE were assessed. The review team reviewed
all deaths, SAEs, discontinuations, and withdrawals.
Trial IB1001-301 was assessed and is reported separately as the only placebo-controlled study of
levacetylleucine in subjects with NPC. Trial IB1001-301 is a cross-over study where subjects
were randomized 1:1 to either levacetylleucine or placebo in addition to their baseline standard
of care for 12 weeks (Treatment Period I), followed by immediate crossover to Treatment
Period II, in which subjects previously on placebo began treatment with levacetylleucine and
subjects previously on levacetylleucine began treatment with Placebo for 12 weeks. Because
there may be carryover effects or repeated AEs (such as worsening of the same AE in the same
subject) from Treatment Period I in Treatment Period II, Treatment Period I was the primary
portion of Trial IB1001-301 reviewed for common ARs. Eligible subjects who completed
Trial IB1001-301 enrolled in an ongoing OLE (IB1001-301) for 2 additional years of treatment,
thus Trial IB1001-301 (including Treatment Period I and Treatment Period II) is referred to as
the “parent study.” All subjects included in the 120-day safety update for Trial IB1001-301 have
completed at least 1 year of open-label treatment (only summary data were available for review
of this ongoing trial).
Trial IB1001-201 was an open-label (single arm) study with a rater-blinded primary efficacy
endpoint. In the parent study, subjects were treated with levacetylleucine for 6 weeks, followed
by a 6-week washout period. Eligible subjects had the option to enroll in an OLE (IB1001-201
OLE) that allowed subjects to continue treatment with levacetylleucine for approximately
2 additional years. IB1001-201 OLE consisted of treatment with levacetylleucine for 1 year
(OLE Treatment Period I), followed by a 6-week washout period, and then a second year of
treatment with levacetylleucine (OLE Treatment Period II).
In addition, the review team separately assessed summary data from an open-label trial of
levacetylleucine in GM2 gangliosidosis, primarily for SAEs, withdrawals, and/or deaths.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The safety database was adequate for a sufficient safety assessment of levacetylleucine (IB1001)
for the indication of NPC in children and adults weighing 15 kg or greater. The review team did
not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough
safety review. No major issues were identified with respect to coding of AEs.
Table 29 shows the baseline demographics of subjects enrolled in Trial IB1001-301, the only
placebo-controlled trial in NPC. The two trial arms were generally well-balanced apart from
there being fewer pediatric subjects in the placebo arm than in the levacetylleucine treatment
arm. There were fewer pediatric participants than adult participants enrolled overall. 23 children

manifestations are expected to be similar across different races and ethnic groups, it is not likely
that care practices and preferences would differ significantly.

7.6. Safety Results
7.6.1. Safety Findings and Concerns, Trial IB1001-301
Overall Summary
The demonstrated safety profile of levacetylleucine is acceptable at the doses intended for
marketing. ARs that occurred in two or more subjects were abdominal pain, dysphagia, upper
respiratory infections, vomiting, and thrombocytopenia on laboratory analysis.
There were no SAEs that led to withdrawal from the trial. There was one death of a subject in
Trial IB1001-301 that occurred after withdrawal from the trial due to complications from a
planned surgical procedure. While the duration of IB1001-301 was relatively short (made up of
two 12-week treatment periods in the crossover study design), the data from this study in
combination with the other submitted safety data for levacetylleucine are adequate to support
84
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
chronic use. The review team concluded that the risks observed are acceptable with appropriate
product labeling and routine pharmacovigilance.
7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Trial IB1001-301
Table 33 provides a summary of AEs reported during the parent study. To avoid the potential
impact of carry over effects from Treatment Period I to Treatment Period II, the risk difference is
only presented for Treatment Period I. One or more TEAEs were reported by more subjects
exposed to levacetylleucine (IB1001) than placebo during Treatment Period I (63% versus 43%,
with a risk difference of 20). In the overall cohort (Treatment Period I and Treatment Period II),
there were two SAEs in one subject while randomized to levacetylleucine, one SAE of which
was ultimately fatal after withdrawal from the trial.

Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
The most reported TEAE in the overall trial population was upper respiratory tract infection,
occurring in 10% of subjects. The most common system organ class with TEAE was infections
and infestations (27%). The most common ARs in the overall population were gastrointestinal
disorders including abdominal pain, dysphagia, and vomiting (See Section 7.7.1). While there
were no reported ARs of thrombocytopenia, laboratory analysis demonstrated thrombocytopenia
in a higher portion of subjects on levacetylleucine compared to placebo during Treatment
Period I (See Section 7.6.1.6).
7.6.1.2. Deaths, Trial IB1001-301
There was one subject with two SAEs unrelated to the study drug that led to withdrawal from the
trial (physician decision) and that ultimately had a fatal outcome during Treatment Period I of
the parent study of Trial IB1001-301.
(b) (6)

Subject
was an adult male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-301. This subject
had severe dystonia requiring multiple medications (gabapentin, trihexiphenidyl, clonazepam
and a THC/CBD oil) in addition to other symptoms of NPC. The subject was status-post
placement of a deep brain stimulator for dystonia and was also taking miglustat. The subject had
a planned elective procedure to place a PEG tube on Study Day 67 (the trial staff was not
previously aware of this planned procedure). The study drug (levacetylleucine) was stopped for
this procedure. After the procedure, the subject experienced an episode of aspiration on Study
Day 70 that lead to pneumonia, and the subject remained hospitalized while being treated with
antibiotics and parenteral nutrition. During this hospitalization the subject also suffered a
neurologic regression with dystonic crises and worsening agitation that progressed to psychosis
before they were withdrawn from the trial by the trial investigator due to their inability to
participate in future trial visits. The subject died from respiratory failure 37 days after being
withdrawn from the trial. The SAEs of aspiration pneumonia and psychosis were both assessed
as not related to the study medication. This reviewer agrees with this assessment.
7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial
IB1001-301
The SAEs reported in Trial IB1001-301 are noted in Table 34. There were three SAEs during the
parent study in subjects randomized to levacetylleucine: two in Treatment Period I (aspiration
pneumonia and psychosis in one subject during the same prolonged hospitalization), and one in
Treatment Period II (incisional hernia repair).

Aspiration Pneumonia and Psychosis
The SAEs that occurred in Treatment Period I (aspiration pneumonia and psychosis) are
described in Section 7.6.1.2. Both occurred in the same subject during a prolonged
hospitalization due to complications of a planned medical procedure after the study drug had
been stopped. Neither event was assessed as related to study drug. This reviewer agrees with this
assessment.
Hernia Repair
(b) (6)

Subject
is an adult male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-301. The subject had a
past medical history of a caecum volvulus and ileus operation in addition to his symptoms of
NPC. They were randomized to placebo during Treatment Period I and then switched to
treatment with levacetylleucine during Treatment Period II. During Treatment Period II (Study
Day 136) the subject developed an abdominal wall hernia secondary to his previous abdominal
surgeries and required surgical repair, which was followed by an 8-day hospitalization. The
subject was discharged home after the procedure on Study Day 144 without incident and
continued the study drug during the hospitalization. This event was assessed as not related to the
study medication. This reviewer agrees with this assessment.
SAEs in Placebo Group During Treatment Period II
Two subjects experienced SAEs while on placebo in Treatment Period II after having received
levacetylleucine during Treatment Period I.
87
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Worsening Epilepsy
(b) (6)

Subject
is an adolescent male at the time of enrollment. The subject had a past medical
history of epilepsy on three anti-seizure medications (levetiracetam, sodium valproate,
clobazam). The subject first experienced an increase in duration and frequency of his seizures
during Treatment Period I, however they did not require hospitalization, thus this was reported as
a severe (i.e., not serious) AE. On Study Day 14 in Treatment Period II, the subject required
hospitalization for control of worsening seizures and medication management. During the
hospitalization, the subject received benzodiazepines and fluids. The subject also had an AE of
aspiration pneumonia during this hospitalization that was attributed to sedation from their
medications causing worsening dysphagia. The subject was discharged home on Study Day 30 of
Treatment Period II. There were no interruptions in the subject’s study drug dosing and this SAE
was assessed as not related to study drug. In the setting of their prior history of treatmentrefractory epilepsy requiring three anti-seizure medications and with the hospitalization
occurring 2 weeks into treatment during Period II while taking placebo, this reviewer concurs
that worsening epilepsy in not likely related to the study drug.
Major Depression
(b) (6)

Subject
was an adult male at the time of trial enrollment. The subject’s past medical
history was significant for a prior psychiatric hospitalization in 2013 in addition to other
symptoms of NPC. The subject was taking sertraline and amantadine daily and clonazepam and
quetiapine as needed for depression. The subject began having worsening symptoms of
depression (moderate) during Treatment Period I, when they were randomized to
levacetylleucine. On the last day of Treatment Period II (during which the subject received
placebo) while being assessed for trial Visit 6, the trial investigator recognized severe symptoms
of depression and determined that urgent hospitalization was needed. Symptoms were described
as similar to catatonia and the subject experienced a significant decline in daily functioning
requiring a prolonged hospitalization. This SAE was assessed as not related to study drug. In the
context of this subject’s prior history of depression requiring hospitalization and worsening over
time (and at the EOT II while on placebo), this reviewer agrees that the SAE major depression is
unlikely related to the study drug.
7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to
Treatment Discontinuation, Trial IB1001-301
There were no AEs that led to treatment discontinuation.
7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial IB1001301
The frequency and severity of TEAEs were evaluated to assess the general safety profile of
levacetylleucine. TEAEs are AEs that developed, worsened, or became serious during the
treatment period. Overall, 58% of subjects experienced a TEAE while on treatment versus 51%
while on placebo. Because there was no washout period between Treatment Period I and
Treatment Period II, the assessment of TEAEs is primarily focused on Treatment Period I, when
there are no potential carryover effects from treatment in the prior period. During Treatment
88

Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Period I. 63% of subjects randomized to levacetylleucine (IB1001) experienced TEAEs
compared to 43% taking placebo.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
During Treatment Period I, TEAEs with a risk difference ≥6.7 were abdominal pain, dysphagia,
nasopharyngitis, upper respiratory tract infections, and vomiting. The review team assessed
frequent TEAEs to be ARs if they could be reasonably explained by the proposed MOA of
levacetylleucine. While it is not clear how levacetylleucine could lead to an increase in upper
respiratory infections directly, upper respiratory infections and nasopharyngitis were included
due to the increased risk for dysphagia and known risk of aspiration in this disorder as it may be
difficult to assess a cause-and-effect relationship of these symptoms during the course of medical
care for NPC while on treatment with levacetylleucine. Seasonal allergies were not considered
ARs due to the seasonal nature of the symptoms, which would be difficult to attribute to any of
the proposed mechanisms of action for levacetylleucine in subjects with NPC.

Treatment-Emergent Events of Interest
AEs reported for the racemate of levacetylleucine, Tanganil, in the label in France (skin rash,
urticaria, hypersensitivity reactions, anaphylactic shock and laryngeal edema and abdominal
pain) as well as those listed in current U.S. labeling for Zavesca (miglustat as approved for adults
with Gaucher disease: peripheral neuropathy, tremor, diarrhea, weight loss, and reductions in
platelet count) were reviewed with heightened interest and included as ARs if they occurred
during treatment with levacetylleucine at a more frequent rate than when treated with placebo.
ARs reported during treatment with levacetylleucine that also have been reported in labeling for
miglustat are gastrointestinal and metabolic symptoms including vomiting, abdominal pain, and
weight loss. One subject who was also taking miglustat experienced an AR of weight loss during
Treatment Period I. There were 4 subjects randomized to levacetylleucine with laboratory
analysis demonstrating a platelet count of <100 in Treatment Period I compared to 2 subjects on
placebo. When comparing decreased platelet counts of the overall trial population during
Treatment Periods I and II, there was a similar occurrence of decreased platelet counts in both
the treatment and placebo groups, which may reflect background miglustat use and/or underlying
disease (see further discussion Laboratory Findings, below). There were more subjects in
Treatment Period I who were randomized to placebo (n=3) who had diarrhea (an AR reported for
miglustat) than those who were randomized to levacetylleucine (n=1). There were no clear
hypersensitivity reactions like those reported with Tanganil in Trial IB1001-301. One subject
93
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
experienced an exacerbation of a previous diagnosis of rosacea during Treatment Period II while
randomized to levacetylleucine that responded to their typical treatment for rosacea
exacerbations and did not require a change in dose or discontinuation of levacetylleucine.
7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial IB1001-301
Routine safety monitoring bloodwork (hematology, clinical chemistry, creatine kinase) and
urinalysis were obtained throughout the trials. The mean values for most of these tests remained
within normal ranges except for low platelets (thrombocytopenia) in both the treatment and
placebo cohorts as detailed below. There were two instances of decreased potassium during
Treatment Period I and one instance of mildly elevated blood urea nitrogen during Treatment
Period II that are not likely CS.
Hematology
Table 37 demonstrates abnormalities in hematology laboratory values during Trial IB1001-301.
There were several isolated low and high white blood cell count values during Period I that are
not likely CS, and most other hematology laboratory values were within normal limits except for
platelets, discussed in further detail in Thrombocytopenia, below.

7.6.1.10. Open-Label Extension, Trial IB1001-301
Eligible subjects who completed Trial IB1001-301 had the option to enroll in the ongoing OLE
(IB1001-301 OLE) for up to 2 years of treatment with levacetylleucine. A total of 54 subjects
entered the EP. The median duration of exposure at the 120-day safety update cut-off was 290
days (62 to 355). Safety information reported for the OLE consists primarily of summary data
because the trial is ongoing.
There have been no deaths reported in the OLE period thus far. There were five SAEs that
occurred in three subjects, none of which were assessed as related to study drug.
One subject was withdrawn after an SAE reported as multi-organ dysfunction due to inability to
participate in future trial visits.
7.6.1.10.1. Serious Adverse Events, Trial IB1001-301 OLE
Three subjects had a total of five SAEs during the OLE. This trial is ongoing and the available
details for each event provided by the Applicant are summarized below.
Multi-Organ Dysfunction
(b) (6)

Subject
was an adult male at the time of enrollment in IB1001-301. On Study Day 269
of the OLE, the subject was hospitalized due to multi-organ failure in the setting of an influenza
A infection. The subject developed bilateral multifocal pneumonia which led to cardiorespiratory
failure requiring cardiopulmonary resuscitation and assisted ventilation. Their hospitalization
was also complicated by bacterial superinfection and septic shock, acute perimoycarditis,
pulmonary edema, and acute renal failure requiring intermittent hemodialysis. The subject had a
tracheostomy and gastrostomy tube placed and a brain computerized tomography scan revealed
brain atrophy. The subject was ultimately withdrawn by the clinical investigator at their trial site
due to inability to participate in future trial visits and was placed on hospice care. The study drug
was stopped at the time of his hospitalization and not restarted thereafter. The SAE of multiorgan dysfunction was assessed as unrelated to the study drug. This reviewer agrees that multiorgan dysfunction was related to the complications of influenza A infection with pneumonia and
not likely related to the study drug.
Urinary Tract Infection, Mycoplasma Pneumonia
(b) (6)

Subject
was an adolescent male at the time of enrollment in IB1001-301. The subject
has a significant medical history of a previous liver transplant in addition to their diagnosis of
NPC with recurrent gastrointestinal hemorrhage, an enlarged spleen, platelet dysfunction,
recurrent diarrhea, and epilepsy. The subject was taking miglustat, ergocalciferol, sodium
valproate, tranexamic acid, and tacrolimus in addition to levacetylleucine during the trial. Six
weeks into the OLE, the subject was hospitalized for a urinary tract infection that was treated
with antibiotics and resolved. No changes to the study drug were made during this
hospitalization.
Approximately 9 months into the OLE (during the first year), the subject was hospitalized with
mycoplasma pneumonia confirmed via chest X-ray and a polymerase chain reaction test for
mycoplasma. The subject was treated with antibiotics first in the hospital and then with an
101
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
outpatient course. The study drug was interrupted during his hospitalization for 3 days and then
restarted at the same dose.
Both SAEs were assessed as not related to the study drug. This reviewer agrees that both the
urinary tract infection and the mycoplasma pneumonia are more likely to be related to the
subject’s underlying disease and not likely related to the study drug.
Seizure
(b) (6)

Subject
was an elementary school age female at the time of enrollment in IB1001-301.
At the time of enrollment, the subject’s relevant past medical history in addition to their
diagnosis of NPC included delayed motor skills, impaired expressive language, ataxia, vertical
gaze palsy, and splenomegaly. The subject was taking miglustat in addition to levacetylleucine
during the trial. After approximately 9 months of open-label treatment with levacetylleucine, the
subject experienced new onset seizures described as “stereotypical movements of the arm with
grimacing and was not responsive to her parents.” An electroencephalogram confirmed seizures
with focal onset. A magnetic resonance image of the subject’s brain was completed and did not
note any specific findings related to her new onset seizures. The subject was hospitalized for
2 days to complete a work-up of new-onset seizures and started on levetiracetam, after which the
AE of “seizure” was considered resolved. No changed to the study drug were made.
Approximately 1 month after her initial diagnosis of epilepsy as detailed above (i.e., after about
10 months of open-label treatment with levacetylleucine), the subject was hospitalized for
management of breakthrough seizures on levetiracetam. The subject was started on lamotrigine
and discharged home after 1 day. No changes were made to the study drug.
The Applicant and investigator assessed both events of seizure as unrelated to the study drug. In
the setting of the subject’s diagnosis of NPC and progressive neurological symptoms as well as
them taking the study drug for >9 months at the time of their initial diagnosis of epilepsy, this
reviewer agrees that the subject’s seizures are more likely due to progression of NPC and less
likely due to levacetylleucine, although because this was a new diagnosis of epilepsy it is not
possible to make definitive conclusions.
7.6.1.10.2. Common Adverse Events, Trial IB1001-301
OLE
Forty-three percent of subjects in the OLE experienced any treatment AE during the OLE. Like
the parent study, the most common system organ classes were infections and infestations
(16.7%) followed by gastrointestinal disorders (13%). Most reported AEs (preferred terms)
occurred just once except for COVID-19 (n=3) and fall (n=2). The recurrence of COVID-19
diagnoses is not unexpected given the incidence in the general population during this time-period
following the worldwide pandemic.
AEs that are also ARs for the concomitant medication miglustat were gastrointestinal disorders
and weight loss. Rash (reported with Tanganil use, which uses a wheat starch excipient) occurred
in two subjects (eczema and rash erythematous).
Data were presented in summary form only for this ongoing trial. Overall, the adverse events
reported in the OLE period were similar to those reported during the parent study and did not
raise any specific new safety concerns.

7.6.2. Safety Results, Trial IB1001-201
Overall Summary
Trial IB1001-201 was an open-label treatment study, thus it did not have a placebo-control group
and was not the primary focus of our safety assessment. A total of 33 subjects with NPC between
the ages of 7 and 64 years were enrolled, of which 31 completed treatment with levacetylleucine
for 6 weeks followed by a 6-week washout period during the parent study. AEs that occurred in
two subjects or more while on treatment with levacetylleucine were diarrhea, seizures, epistaxis,
fall, rash, rhinitis, and upper respiratory tract infections. All these AEs were also reported in at
least one subject during the placebo-controlled Trial IB1001-301 and remained relatively
infrequent, thus no new specific treatment related risks were identified.
There were no SAEs that led to withdrawal from the trial. There were no deaths during the parent
study. There was one withdrawal due to worsening epilepsy that was reported as mild in intensity
and assessed as not related to the study drug.
7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Trial IB1001-201
Table 43 provides a summary of AEs reported during the parent study, Trial IB1001-201. One or
more TEAEs were reported by more subjects exposed to levacetylleucine (IB1001) than during
the washout period (70% versus 21%). In the overall cohort, there were six SAEs in four subjects
and no deaths.

Lower Respiratory Tract Infection
(b) (6)

Subject
was an adolescent male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-201. The
subject’s past medical history was relevant for prior gastrostomy tube placement, sialorrhea,
epilepsy, cataplexy, and recurrent respiratory infection in addition to their diagnosis of NPC. The
subject was hospitalized on Study Day 30 for a lower respiratory tract infection that required
intravenous antibiotics and resolved on Study Day 39. The study drug was continued throughout
the hospitalization. This event was assessed as not related to the study medication by the clinical
investigator. This reviewer agrees that the AE of lower respiratory tract infection is likely related
to progression of NPC, sialorrhea, and recurrent lung infection.
Dehydration
(b) (6)

Subject
was an elementary school age male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001201. The subject’s relevant past medical history besides a diagnosis of NPC is significant for a
liver transplant, chronic gastrointestinal inflammation, recurrent gastrointestinal hemorrhage, and
anemia. On Study Day 23, the subject developed a “chest” infection treated with amoxicillin and
was hospitalized for dehydration for 24 hours on Study Day 26. The study drug was interrupted
during the hospital stay only and restarted at the same dose. The SAE of dehydration was
assessed as unrelated to the study drug and attributed to amoxicillin by the clinical investigator.
It is difficult to determine whether amoxicillin was the culprit based on the information provided;
however, this reviewer agrees that the hospitalization was likely related to either the infection or
the associated treatment, not the study drug.
Fall With Head Injury and Rib Fracture
(b) (6)

Subject
was an adult female at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-201. The
subject had a history of previous recurrent falls. The subject had a fall that resulted in a closed
head injury and multiple rib fractures resulting in a hospitalization from Study Day 44 to Study
Day 50. The study drug was interrupted for 6 days during the hospitalization. The event was
reported as resolved 1 month later. This SAE was assessed as not related to the study drug
because there were no features suggesting a drug-related cause (e.g., new onset hypotension).
This reviewer agrees that a traumatic fall was unlikely related to the study drug and more likely
related to symptoms of ataxia or abnormal gait due to NPC or an unrelated accidental fall.
7.6.2.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to
Treatment Discontinuation, Trial IB1001-201
One subject discontinued the trial due to worsening epilepsy (seizures increased).
(b) (6)

Subject
was an adolescent male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-201.
Relevant medical history besides a diagnosis of NPC was epilepsy requiring three antiseizure
medications (ethosuximide, valproate, and clobazam). The subject had additional seizures
reported as “mild” on Study Day 10. The subject was treated with three additional antiseizure
medications with some improvement but was ultimately withdrawn from the trial at the
discretion of the clinical investigator on Study Day 36. This reviewer agrees that the increase in
seizures of mild intensity was unlikely to be related to the study drug and more likely related to
progression of NPC with treatment-refractory epilepsy.

During treatment with levacetylleucine, the AEs that occurred most were diarrhea and seizure in
three subjects, followed by fall, lower respiratory tract infection, rash pruritic, rhinitis, and upper
respiratory infection, which occurred in two subjects each. Diarrhea, seizure, fall and rhinitis all
occurred in one subject each during the washout period as well. All other AEs occurred in one
subject each. These TEAEs are generally similar to those seen in Trial IB1001-301. Because the
parent study of Trial IB1001-201 was both an open-label study and of shorter duration than the
pivotal trial, it would be challenging to determine whether any of the AEs that occurred
infrequently during Trial IB1001-201 and only once during Trial IB1001-301 are more likely to
be attributable to levacetylleucine rather than NPC or concomitant medications. Thus, labeling
will reflect the AEs identified during the double-blind placebo-controlled trial during Treatment
Period I.
7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial IB1001-201
Routine safety monitoring bloodwork (hematology, clinical chemistry, creatine kinase, liver
function analyses) and urinalysis were obtained. The mean values for most of these tests
remained within normal ranges except for platelet levels, which were in the range of
thrombocytopenia both during treatment with levacetylleucine and during the washout period.
Hematology
Table 46 demonstrates abnormalities in hematology laboratory values during Trial IB1001-201.
Most of the hematology laboratory values were within normal limits except for platelet levels,
which revealed thrombocytopenia (<150 10^3 cells/uL) both during open-label treatment with
levacetylleucine and during the washout period.

Thrombocytopenia
There was a similar number of subjects during the treatment and washout periods with
thrombocytopenia. During the treatment period, 14/33 (42%) subjects had platelets
<140 10^3 cells/uL compared to 12/31 (39%) during the washout period. There were 8/33 (24%)
compared to 7/31 (23%) with platelets <125 10^3 cells/uL, and 2 subjects during the treatment
period and 1 subject during the washout period with platelets <100 10^3 cells/uL. Because
patients with NPC can have thrombocytopenia and because thrombocytopenia is also a known
risk of miglustat, it is not clear if there is an increased risk of thrombocytopenia attributable to
levacetylleucine from this data. Because this risk was similarly demonstrated in Trial IB1001301, these results from Trial IB101-201 did not raise additional safety concerns.
7.6.2.7. Open-Label Extension, Trial IB1001-201
Eligible subjects who completed Trial IB1001-201 had the option to enroll in an OLE (IB1001201 OLE) for up to 2 years of treatment with levacetylleucine. A total of 19 subjects entered the
EP, of which 13 subjects completed the trial. There were two open-label treatment periods,
Treatment Period I consisted of 1 year of open-label treatment, followed by a 6-week washout
period, and then Treatment Period II, which consisted of a second year of open-label treatment.
The total duration of exposure during the OLE ranged from 176 to 816 days.

There were no deaths reported in the OLE. There were 22 SAEs reported in five subjects, none

the SAEs reported occurred in one subject who had multiple complications of NPC during the

Applicant.

The most common AEs that occurred during either of the 1-year-long open-label treatment

reported during the first year of open-label treatment (8) compared to the second year of
open-label treatment (2). Because there is no control group for comparison, conclusions
regarding increased frequency of falls with prolonged treatment could not be drawn.
Generally, the AEs reported during both the parent study and OLE of Trial IB1001-201 were
similar to those reported during Trial IB1001-301 with the exception of multiple coronavirus
infections, which reflects the timing of the trial during the coronavirus pandemic. There were
no additional new safety concerns raised during the OLE of Trial IB1001-201.

7.6.3. Safety Results, Other Indications
7.6.3.1. Trial IB1001-202
Summary
Trial IB1001-202, an open-label treatment study in GM2 gangliosidosis, was reviewed only
for SAEs, withdrawals, and deaths. GM2 gangliosidosis is another autosomal recessive
lysosomal storage disorder with some overlapping signs and symptoms seen in NPC.
In general, levacetylleucine was well-tolerated in Trial IB1001-202 and there were no SAEs,
AEs leading to withdrawal from the trial, or deaths that raised any new safety concerns in our
review of levacetylleucine.
Trial IB1001-202, Parent Study
A total of 30 subjects between the ages of 6 and 55 years were enrolled, of which 27 subjects
completed treatment with levacetylleucine for 6 weeks followed by a 6-week washout period
during the parent study. The duration of levacetylleucine exposure in the parent study ranged
from 16 to 132 days.
There were four SAEs that occurred in two subjects, none of which were assessed as related
to the study drug. There were no deaths during the parent study. There was one withdrawal
after 16 days of treatment with levacetylleucine for tremor without any further follow-up for
an assessment and determination of causation, and one withdrawal due to COVID-19
pandemic-related travel restrictions.
SAEs that occurred during Trial IB1001-201 include a fall causing a vertebral fracture in one
subject and an exacerbation of previously diagnosed bipolar disorder with psychotic features
in another subject; neither of these SAEs were assessed as related to the study drug.
IB1001-202 Open-Label Extension
Subjects who completed the parent study were eligible to enroll in an open-label extension
study for up to 2 additional years of open-label treatment with a 6-week washout period in
between the two open-label treatment periods. Fourteen subjects enrolled in the EP, and just
seven subjects completed both years of treatment. The duration of levacetylleucine exposure
during the OLE ranged from 266 to 754 days.
Like Trial IB1001-201, this trial coincided with the emergence of the COVID-19 pandemic,
and there were multiple withdrawals from the trial with limited details regarding the reason
for the withdrawal.
116
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
There was one SAE reported in one subject during the OLE. This SAE was an exacerbation
of bipolar disorder that occurred during the washout period of the OLE and assessed as not
related to the study drug. There were no TEAEs that led to withdrawal from the trial. There
were no deaths.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Embryofetal Development Toxicity in Rodents
and Non-Rodents
Issue
Dose-range finding animal embryo-fetal development (EFD) toxicity studies in rats and
rabbits demonstrate EFD toxicity including induction of malformations following
administration of levacetylleucine during pregnancy. However, final study reports for EFD
toxicity studies in rats and rabbits have not been submitted. Additionally, because the
protocols for EFD toxicity studies in rats and rabbits were not submitted to the agency, it is
uncertain if the doses that were selected for definitive studies were adequate to evaluate the
risk to EFD.
Background
In pregnant rats, increases in resorptions, post-implantation loss, and skeletal malformation
were seen due to exposure to levacetylleucine at doses of 600 mg/kg/day and at the
human-equivalent dose of 97 mg/kg/day. In pregnant rabbits, increased resorptions,
post-implantation loss, decreased fetal body weight, and increased skeletal malformations
were observed following exposure to levacetylleucine at doses of 1250 mg/kg/day and at the
human-equivalent dose of 403 mg/kg/day.
Assessment
Concern for a CS risk to EFD was demonstrated in animal studies. Specifically, nonclinical
effects occurred in both nonclinical species. A Warning and Precaution for EFD toxicity that
describes the potential risk to a fetus with recommendations to mitigate the risk (i.e., effective
contraception) should be included in labeling. In addition, information was also described in
Section 8.1 Pregnancy and in Section 8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential
subsections of the label (Table 89). See Section 8.4 of this review for details.
Conclusion
A Warning and Precaution for EFD toxicity recommends that females of reproductive
potential avoid pregnancy and use effective contraception during treatment with
levacetylleucine. Submission of final study reports for EFD toxicity studies in rats and rabbits
will be required as a postmarketing requirement (PMR). Additionally, because it is uncertain
if the doses and trial design for definitive studies were appropriate for reasons described
above, the Applicant may be required to repeat the EFD toxicity studies in rats and rabbits
after the dose selection and trial protocols have been reviewed by the Agency.

117
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

7.7.2. Juvenile Toxicology Profile Unknown
Issue
A juvenile toxicity study with levacetylleucine has not been conducted. Evaluation of toxicity
of levacetylleucine in juvenile animals is needed because onset of NPC ranges from infancy
into adulthood, and treatment with levacetylleucine may be lifelong. Data in the rat and rabbit
EFD studies indicate levacetylleucine crossed the placenta and modulated growth of bones,
and it is expected that levacetylleucine will modulate the growth of bones in juvenile animals
as well. Because levacetylleucine crosses the blood brain barrier and because its major
metabolite, leucine, causes defects in ossification of the skull, torsion of the neck, and
depression of the brain waves (Bergstrom et al. 1967), levacetylleucine has the potential to
influence growth, neurological development (learning and memory), sexual development, and
general health. Hence, there is need for a juvenile toxicity study to determine the toxic effects
of levacetylleucine in juvenile animals and to inform patients of potential risks of this
medication. These risks cannot be readily characterized in patients in the postmarketing
setting.
Conclusion
While the current clinical safety and efficacy data appear to be adequate to support approval
in pediatric patients, the available data are limited; therefore, a juvenile toxicity study will be
required as a PMR.

7.7.3. Effects on Male and Female Fertility and Early
Embryonic Development Unknown
Issue
Rodent male and female fertility and early embryonic development toxicity studies with
levacetylleucine has not been conducted. Evaluation of effects of levacetylleucine on fertility
and early embryonic development is needed because there are adult-onset forms of NPC and
because treatment with levacetylleucine for early onset NPC may continue into reproductive
age. As such, some patients of reproductive age are likely to be exposed. Additionally,
levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid that appears to have widespread distribution;
therefore, placental transfer is expected, and there may be an impact on embryo implantation,
as suggested by data from the non-good laboratory practice dose-ranging embryofetal
development studies that were submitted in the NDA. Levacetylleucine appeared to reduce
the number of implantations in dams treated in an EFD study; therefore, the purpose of this
study is to evaluate whether levacetylleucine may affect fertility through inhibition of
implantation or through other effects on gametogenesis.
Conclusion
Information was described in the impairment of fertility subsection of labeling (Table 89;
Section 13.1) to indicate that studies of fertility and early embryonic development and preand postnatal toxicology were not conducted with the drug. These risks cannot be readily
characterized in patients. Rodent male and female fertility and early embryonic development
toxicity studies will be required as PMRs to support risk communication to patients and
caregivers.

118
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

7.7.4. Effects on Pre- and Postnatal Development
Unknown
Issue
A rodent pre- and postnatal development toxicity study with levacetylleucine has not been
conducted. Evaluation of the effects of levacetylleucine on pre- and postnatal development is
needed because onset of NPC ranges from infancy into adulthood, and treatment with
levacetylleucine may begin in infancy and be lifelong. Levacetylleucine was found to cause
increases in post-implantation loss and malformation in skeletal bones in a dose-range finding
EFD study in rabbits. Leucine, a metabolite of levacetylleucine, as a single 0.2 mL injection
of a 1% solution in the amnionic sac of a nine-day-old chicken embryo is reported to cause
morphological anomalies of the legs (including inward flexion of the toes, leg extension or
flexion at hip joint), defects in ossification of the skull, torsion of the neck, and depression of
the brain waves (Bergstrom et al. 1967). Therefore, exposure to levacetylleucine has the
potential to cause adverse effects on growth and development (neurological, physical, and
sexual) from prenatal and into postnatal stages of life. This possibility requires investigation.
Additionally, levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid that appears to have widespread
distribution; therefore, placental and lactational transfer are expected. Significant exposure to
a fetus and/or neonate is likely. This study will evaluate the effect of the drug on growth,
maturation, and reproduction when offspring are exposed during pregnancy and lactation.
This risk cannot be readily characterized in patients.
Conclusion
Information was described in the impairment of fertility subsection of labeling (Table 89;
Section 8.1) to indicate that study of pre- and postnatal toxicology has not been conducted. A
rodent pre- and postnatal development toxicity study will be required as a PMR.

7.7.5. Carcinogenic Risk Unknown
Issue
Carcinogenicity studies with levacetylleucine have not been conducted. Leucine, a metabolite
of levacetylleucine, is known to activate mammalian target of rapamycin (mTOR) signaling
and promotes pancreatic tumor growth in mice (Liu et al. 2014). Due to the role of leucine in
promotion of tumors, it is likely that exposure to levacetylleucine and to leucine as a
metabolite may increase carcinogenic risk in humans. Levacetylleucine is intended for
lifetime administration beginning in infancy or childhood. Because potential neoplastic
effects may not manifest in patients for many years, the risk cannot be evaluated in a timely
manner in humans. Therefore, carcinogenicity studies are needed to determine potential
carcinogenicity risk to patients.
Conclusion
Information was described in the carcinogenesis subsection of labeling to indicate that animal
carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with the drug. A 26-week carcinogenicity
study in the transgenic rasH2 mouse will be required as a PMR, along with a 2-year rat
carcinogenicity study. Alternatively, the Agency may accept a weight-of-evidence evaluation
in lieu of conducting the study if the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee
agrees that the study will not add value. However, to date, the Applicant has not submitted a
justification for a weight-of-evidence evaluation.
119
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

7.7.6. Concomitant Miglustat Use
Issue
The high degree of concomitant miglustat use in Trial IB1001-301 impacts the determination
of risk for levacetylleucine.
Background
Miglustat is considered standard of care for patients with NPC by treating clinicians.
Miglustat is approved for the progressive neurological symptoms of NPC in other countries
and is used off-label in the United States. The percentage of patients with concomitant use of
miglustat in Trial IB1001-301 was 87%.
The current labeling for miglustat contains ‘Warnings and Precautions’ for the following:
tremor, gastrointestinal events such as diarrhea and weight loss, peripheral neuropathy, and
thrombocytopenia. Similar findings, particularly of gastrointestinal symptoms, were
identified in IB1001-301, making interpretation of AEs strictly related to levacetylleucine
challenging.
Assessment
The assessment of the safety of levacetylleucine in patients diagnosed with NPC is based on
the comparison to placebo. However, the current standard of care in this multinational trial
includes the use of miglustat, which was used by most trial participants in both treatment
arms at their baseline dose during the trial. AEs that occurred in at least two subjects or more
while on treatment with levacetylleucine in the placebo-controlled parent Trial IB1001-301
include the following: abdominal pain, dysphagia, upper respiratory infections, and vomiting.
Labeling will reflect these AEs. Several of these AEs overlap with those currently listed on
the miglustat label, so it is difficult to determine if these events were attributable to
levacetylleucine.
Conclusion
As the number of enrolled subjects not treated with concomitant miglustat is low, it is
difficult to make a comparison of those receiving levacetylleucine with or without miglustat.
The data provide an assessment of levacetylleucine, with most subjects on background
miglustat. While there are limitations in attributing certain AEs to either miglustat or
levacetylleucine (which appeared to have some similarities in safety profile), the data are
adequate to support the safety of levacetylleucine with and without background miglustat use.

7.7.7. Assessment of Safety in Patients <10 Years
Old (≥15 kg)
Issue/Background
Trial IB1001-301, the only placebo-controlled trial in the levacetylleucine development
program, enrolled seven (11.7%) subjects who were <10 years old; the youngest subject was
5 years old. There were two (6.1%) subjects <10 years old in the open-label Trial IB1001201, and six (20%) subjects <10 years old in the open-label study for another indication
(GM2 gangliosidosis). The levacetylleucine development program did not include any
subjects who were <5 years old for NPC or any other indication. The inclusion criteria for
120
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
≥15 kg. Routine pharmacovigilance will be adequate to continue assessment of an acceptable
risk profile in the approved population.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
The recommended dosage regimen of levacetylleucine in patients with NPC is based on body
weight, which is the approach used in the clinical trials of levacetylleucine. Body weight was
identified as a significant covariate affecting levacetylleucine PK in the PopPK analyses. The
currently available data do not support a need for further therapeutic individualization based
on other intrinsic factors.
Body Weight
Body weight was identified as a significant covariate on apparent clearance and apparent
volume of distribution in the PopPK analysis (see Section 14.5). The apparent clearance and
apparent volume of distribution of levacetylleucine increases with increasing body weight.
These effects on PK of levacetylleucine are minimized by the recommended weight-based
dosing regimen.
Sex
Sex was not identified as a significant covariate in the PopPK analysis, and therefore, not
expected to have CS effect on levacetylleucine exposures.
Subjects With NPC vs. Healthy Subjects
PK data were not available for healthy subjects.
Hepatic Impairment or Renal Impairment
The effects of renal impairment and hepatic impairment on the PK of levacetylleucine have
not been studied. No mass balance study has been conducted with levacetylleucine in
humans. The metabolism of levacetylleucine results in the formation of acetate and L-leucine,
and both byproducts are used within the body in further catabolic pathways. One study
demonstrated that after an intravenous dose of 14C-L-leucine to rats, 5% of the substrate was
excreted in urine as levacetylleucine (Lee et al. 2015). Thus, the impact of hepatic
impairment on PK of levacetylleucine is expected to be low.
Patients with NPC with later onset primarily present with neurological symptoms. While
some patients with NPC may exhibit liver organomegaly, significant liver impairment is
generally not observed in this disease (Geberhiwot et al. 2018). Trials IB1001-201 and
IB1001-301 did not include specific inclusion/exclusion criteria for liver function. In these
trials, the liver function status of subjects (i.e., normal, mild, moderate, or severe) was not
explicitly assessed using tools such as the Child-Pugh classification, Mayo risk score, or a
comprehensive set of hepatic function markers. However, based on bilirubin levels, which is
a key indicator of liver function, as well as other liver blood chemistry parameters (AST,
ALT, alkaline phosphatase) monitored throughout the trials, the subjects generally appeared
to have normal liver function. This absence of specific liver function assessments limits the
ability to correlate liver function status with PK parameters.
122
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Drug-Drug Interaction
Levacetylleucine was found to not inhibit cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes or induce
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 enzymes via in vitro studies, indicating a low risk for drugdrug interactions through these pathways. Although levacetylleucine is identified as a
substrate of organic anion transporter 1 and OAT3, these interactions are not considered
clinically relevant due to the established safety profile and minimal renal excretion of
levacetylleucine. Breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) - and P-glycoprotein (P-gp)mediated levacetylleucine active transport was not detected. In addition, the Applicant
assessed the potential for levacetylleucine to inhibit efflux transporters (e.g., P-gp, BCRP,
and bile salt export pump; see Section 14). The risk associated with bile salt export pump
inhibition is low due to the Cmax/IC50 of <0.1. The risk of drug-drug interactions due to BCRP
inhibition is also considered low, as the Igut/IC50 is slightly higher than the accepted ratio
of 10. However, the potential for P-gp inhibition in vivo cannot be ruled out, given the
significantly elevated Igut/IC50. Consequently, the review team recommends more frequent
monitoring for ARs related to P-gp substrates in the label, as well as a PMR trial to further
evaluate the potential interactions with P-gp substrates.
Food Effect
The effect of food on PK of the to-be-marketed formulation was not assessed in a dedicated
clinical trial. Based on the clinical trial protocols, levacetylleucine was supposed to be
administered to subjects with NPC without food (defined as the drug needing to be taken
either 30 minutes before a meal or at least 2 hours after a meal). However, approximately
12.5% non-compliance incidents (subjects took the drug within 2 hours after a meal) were
reported in the clinical trials. The review team conducted a preliminary PK analysis to
compare the drug concentration during the first 2.5 hours postdose under “fasted” (subjects
took the drug following the protocols regarding food intake) and fed (subjects took the drug
non-compliant with the protocols regarding food intake) conditions (see Section 14). Based
on this assessment, similar PK during the absorption phase was observed regardless of food
intake. However, due to the small sample size of subjects who had intensive PK data that
enabled this analysis and uncontrolled food conditions (i.e., high fat or low fat), these
findings cannot support a definitive conclusion. Because patients with NPC often experience
feeding difficulties and may require a long time to complete meals, enforcing the
administration of the drug without food for a TID dosing regimen would be challenging and
burdensome for these patients (i.e., patients will need to fast for 2.5 hours). Therefore, based
on the preliminary PK analysis and practical considerations, the review team recommends the
drug to be taken with or without food in the label. To address the uncertainty associated with
the impact of food on PK, a postmarketing commitment trial has been proposed.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (21 U.S.C. 355c), all applications for new active
ingredients (which includes new salts and new fixed combinations), new indications, new
dosage forms, new dosing regimens, or new routes of administration are required to contain
an assessment of the safety and effectiveness of the product for the claimed indication in
pediatric patients unless this requirement is waived, deferred, or inapplicable.

123
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Because this drug product for this indication has an orphan drug designation, it is exempt
from this requirement.
The currently available data support the approval of this product in patients weighing ≥15 kg.
Nonclinical
There are no nonclinical data to support the safety of levacetylleucine in children; however,
available clinical data suggest that the drug is adequately tolerated. A juvenile animal study
will be conducted as a PMR to assess target organ effects and effects on developmental
processes including neurocognitive development, growth, and sexual maturation.
Clinical
The safety and effectiveness of levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC have been
established in 23 pediatric subjects weighing ≥15 kg in Trial IB1001-301. Use of
levacetylleucine for this indication is supported by evidence from one adequate and wellcontrolled trial in adult and pediatric subjects weighing ≥15 kg with additional
pharmacokinetic data from 40 adult and 17 pediatric subjects who participated in two openlabel studies. The safety and effectiveness of levacetylleucine have not been established in
pediatric subjects weighing <15 kg. See Section 7.7.7 for additional information regarding
the safety assessment of pediatric subjects weighing ≥15 kg.

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males
of Reproductive Potential
Animal Data
In a dose-ranging rat EFD study, doses of up to 1000 mg/kg/day oral levacetylleucine were
administered to pregnant females during organogenesis (gestation day [GD] 6 through
GD 17). Increases in the mean number of resorptions, mean post-implantation losses, and
skeletal malformations (thoracic arch of the vertebra-fused thoracic arch, misaligned thoracic
arch, hemicentric thoracic centrum, and misaligned thoracic centrum; vertebral columnscoliosis, and ribs-absent costal cartilage and short rib) were observed at doses
≥600 mg/kg/day, which is 1.4-fold the MRHD of IB1001 (levacetylleucine) based on body
surface area.
In a dose-ranging rabbit EFD study, doses of up to 2500 mg/kg/day oral levacetylleucine
were administered daily to pregnant females during organogenesis (GD 7 through GD 19).
Increased embryo-fetal death (post-implantation loss/resorption), decrease in fetal body
weight, and increase in external (open or partially open eyes, hyperflexion of limbs) and
skeletal malformations (misshapen maxilla and premaxilla, and small interparietal bones)
were observed at the ≥1250 mg/kg/day, which is 6-fold the MRHD of levacetylleucine based
on body surface area.

9. Product Quality
Drug Substance
Levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid; it is acetyl amide of the amino acid L-leucine.
Levacetylleucine has not been previously approved or marketed in the United States, and
therefore, it is classified as a new molecular entity.

III. Additional Analyses and

12. Summary of Regulatory History
The Applicant, IntraBio Inc, has investigated levacetylleucine, also referred to by the
company development code IB1001 and by its chemical name N-acetyl-L-leucine (NALL),
for its ability to treat Niemann-Pick disease type C (NPC). The Applicant’s development
program began with the investigation of commercially available racemic mixture N-acetylDL-leucine, a modified amino acid approved in France since 1957 as Tanganil (Pierre Fabre
Laboratories) for short term (10 days to 6 weeks) treatment of acute vertigo in adults. The
Applicant opened a pre-investigational new drug application (PIND) file (PIND 134369) and
submitted a PIND meeting request on February 22, 2017, to discuss their development
program for N-acetyl-DL-leucine. The meeting was granted by the Agency; however, the
Applicant cancelled the meeting on April 13, 2017 because it determined the program needed
additional refinement before any discussions should occur. The Applicant also received an
orphan drug designation for N-acetyl-DL-leucine for the treatment of NPC on February 22,
2018.
The Applicant resubmitted a PIND meeting request on February 26, 2018, this time to discuss
their nonclinical and clinical plan to develop levacetylleucine, the L-enantiomer and active
ingredient of the racemic mixture of N-acetyl-DL-leucine. The Applicant asserted that animal
studies suggested that the L-enantiomer had a greater potential clinical benefit over the
racemic mixture. The Applicant intended to formulate levacetylleucine as granules for oral
(b) (4)
suspension for the treatment of NPC
In the PIND meeting, held on
July 17, 2018, the FDA commented on the Applicant’s proposed plan to conduct a phase
2/2b, open-label clinical trial to assess the safety and efficacy of levacetylleucine. The FDA
asserted that an open-label trial could be used for proof-of-concept, but that it would be
inadequate to generate interpretable efficacy data to support a future marketing application.
General comments were also provided, particularly for nonclinical studies, primary endpoints
considerations, the statistical analysis approach, and the clinical pharmacology development
(b) (4)
plan. In addition, the FDA advised the Applicant
(b) (4)
The meeting minutes were issued on July 26,
2018.
On January 7, 2019, IND 134369 was submitted and included protocol IB1001-201 titled
“Effects of N-Acetyl-L-Leucine on Niemann-Pick Disease Type C: A Multinational,
Multicenter, Open-Label, Rater-Blinded Phase II Study.” Per the FDA’s request, a
teleconference took place on January 23, 2019. In the teleconference, the FDA reiterated the
concerns regarding the limitations of an open-label trial design versus a randomized,
controlled trial and reminded the Applicant that Trial IB1001-201, as designed, would only
provide short term safety information. The Sponsor acknowledged the FDA’s concerns and
stated that Trial IB101-201 was an exploratory study and agreed to revise the protocol per
FDA’s recommendation to eliminate the open-label extension and limit the trial to 6 weeks in
duration. The FDA issued a Study May Proceed letter on February 7, 2019. The FDA
recommended the Applicant request a guidance meeting to discuss improving the trial design
(e.g., incorporating randomization) and to further discuss some of the trial’s key elements
(e.g., the clinical outcome assessment endpoints and statistical approach). General

as a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, crossover study that the

the proposed randomized study design for Trial IB1001-301 appeared acceptable, the FDA
reminded the Applicant to address some of its previously communicated concerns, such as

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary of Review of Studies Submitted
With the Investigational New Drug
Application
The nonclinical safety profile of IB1001 (also referred as N-acetyl-L-leucine, NALL, and
levacetylleucine) supporting this NDA was evaluated in: (1) repeated dose toxicity studies in
rats (up to 6 months) and dogs (up to 9 months); (2) a standard battery of in vitro and in vivo
genotoxicity studies; and (3) dose-ranging reproductive and developmental toxicity studies
including embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) studies in rats and rabbits. Additional
nonclinical studies, including fertility studies in male and female rats, peri- and postnatal
developmental study in rats, carcinogenicity studies in transgenic mice (6 months) and rats
(2-year bioassay). Juvenile toxicology studies in rats were not included in this submission.
Given the imminently life-threatening nature of NPC and the high unmet medical need, these
studies will be requested as postmarketing requirements (PMRs).

130
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
The following study was previously reviewed under the IND:
Study Number 36243: 6-Week Subchronic Toxicity Study of IB1001 by Repeated Oral
Administration to Beagle Dogs
Purpose: Evaluate oral toxicity following daily dosing and reversibility of effects in dogs.
Design: Daily oral dosing in Beagle dogs (3M/3F per dose level + 2M/2F in the high dose
and control groups) dosed at 0, 225, 750, or 2500 mg/kg/day for 6 weeks followed by a
recovery period of 2 weeks. Body weight, feed consumption, electrocardiography (ECG),
blood pressure, ophthalmological and auditory examinations, clinical pathology,
histopathology, and toxicokinetics (TKs) were evaluated.
Key Findings: There were no adverse effects at up to 2500 mg/kg/day. The maximum
plasma concentration (Cmax) and area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) levels of
levacetylleucine were dose-related after Days 1 and 42 of dosing. The combined male and
female Cmax was 252.8 µg/mL and AUC0-∞ was 598 µg.hr/mL at the no-observed-adverseeffect level (NOAEL) on Day 42.
Conclusion: The NOAEL for levacetylleucine was 2500 mg/kg/day in a 6-week oral gavage
toxicity study in Beagle dogs. The NOAEL dose in dog provides a 19-fold safety margin
based on exposures in humans (31.6 µg·hr/mL) at a daily maximum recommended human
dose of 4 g/day.

13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted
With the New Drug Application
13.2.1. Nonclinical Pharmacodynamic Data
Data to support the mechanism of action for levacetylleucine were derived from published
literature. The Applicant described data suggesting that in cultured Chinese hamster ovary
cells, treatment with levacetylleucine at a concentration of 1mM for 24 hours, was associated
with a modest reduction in lysosomal volume, sphingosine, glycosphingolipids (GSLs), and a
very modest reduction in stored cholesterol. In NPC patient-derived fibroblasts, treatment
with levacetylleucine was also associated with a modest reduction in apparent lysosomal
volume when cells were cultured for 7 days with 1mM levacetylleucine (Mann 2018). These
studies suggest that levacetylleucine has the potential to reduce stored intracellular lipid;
however, the mechanism of action for this drug, including the molecular targets by which it
elicits these effects, is unclear.
The Applicant also provided published data from an animal disease model. In Npc1-/- mice,
pre-symptomatic (from the age of 3 weeks old) treatment with levacetylleucine was also
associated with a modest increase in survival and a reduction in markers of
neuroinflammation (CD68 staining) in the cerebellum (Section 13.2.2; (Kaya et al. 2021).
The above changes were not monitored in the clinical trial to evaluate the association of
change in magnitude of levels of GSL and neuroinflammation with the dose of
levacetylleucine and efficacy. At the Division’s request, the Applicant provided raw data
obtained by the publication’s authors, and a letter of reference granting them the right to use
the raw data (submission dated July 3, 2024) and revised right to use raw data certifying that
spreadsheets accurately reflect the data contained in publication by Kaya et al. 2021. The
figures in sections below are obtained from Kaya et al. 2021 in sequence #0050 (submission
dated July 25, 2024). The figures in sections below are obtained from Kaya et al. 2021 with
