In Vitro Mechanism of Action
Study of NALL Treatment on Lysosomal Volume and Accumulation of Sphingosines In
Vitro
The effects of NALL on lysosomal volume and levels of sphingosine, GSLs, and free
cholesterol were measured in cultured wildtype and NPC1-deficient (Npc1-/-) Chinese
hamster ovary cells. Treatment of Npc1-/- Chinese hamster ovary cells with 1mM NALL
reduced lysosomal volume compared to untreated cells after 24 hours (16%; p=0.0177) and
72 hours of treatment (22.8%; p=0.0036) (Figure 14 panel A). Levacetylleucine reduced
sphingosine levels by 29% compared to untreated cells (p<0.0001). Additionally, NALL
significantly reduced cholesterol content by 13% compared to untreated cells (p=0.0263)
(Figure 14 panel B).

Study of NALL Treatment on Lysosomal Volume in Fibroblasts From Patients With
NPC
The effect of 1mM levacetylleucine on fibroblasts from patients with NPC with severe
clinical presentation was evaluated by analyzing change in LysoTracker fluorescence
associated with change in lysosomal volume. The treatment of fibroblasts from patients with
NPC with levacetylleucine for 7 days modestly reduced the LysoTracker fluorescence,
suggesting that the drug reduced lysosomal volume compared to untreated fibroblasts from
patients with NPC. There was no numerical difference in the magnitude of the effect when
cells were treated with levacetylleucine versus when they were treated with N-acetylDL-leucine.

In Vivo Mechanism of Action
Study of NALL Treatment on Lipid Storage in Brain in Npc1-/- Mice
The effect of administering levacetylleucine in pre-symptomatic Npc1-/- mice beginning at the
age of 3 weeks old, was evaluated by measuring lipid storage in brain. The Npc1-/- mice
exhibited an increase in total GSL levels in forebrain and cerebellum compared to wild type
mice at 59 days (approximately 8.5 weeks) of age (Figure 16 panels A and B; Kaya et al.
2021). Treatment of Npc1-/- mice with levacetylleucine at 0.1 mg/kg/day modestly reduced
(not significantly) the total GSL levels compared to untreated Npc1-/- mice. The reduction in
certain GSLs was more pronounced with reduction in GM1a (20.1%; p=0.0018) and GM2
(19.6%; p=0.0222) in the forebrain and the reduction of GA2 (23.8%; p=0.0042) and GA1
(23.5%; p=0.0254) in cerebellum.

Study of NALL Treatment on Cerebellar Ataxia Motor Skills and Long-Term
Neuroprotective Effect in Pre-Symptomatic Npc1-/- Mice.
The Applicant states that the lifespan of Npc1-/- mice is about 10 to 12 weeks with onset of
symptoms, such as gait abnormalities, tremor, and weight loss beginning at between the ages
of 6 and 7 weeks old. The Applicant states that at 9 weeks of age, untreated Npc1-/- mice
show statistically significant ataxia that presents as pronounced sigmoid gait compared to
wildtype Npc1-/- mice (p<0.0001). Ataxia was determined by measuring lateral displacement
from a straight trajectory in an automated gait analysis system (Figure 17 panels A and B).
Kaya et al. 2021 reported that treatment of Npc1-/- mice with 100 mg/kg/day of
levacetylleucine for 1 week beginning at the age of 8 weeks old showed significant reduction
in ataxia (p<0.0001) compared to untreated Npc1-/- mice. In mice treated pre-symptomatically
from weaning (i.e., at the age of 3 weeks old) and assessed at the age of 9 weeks old (i.e.,
after 6 weeks of treatment), levacetylleucine also significantly reduced the magnitude of
ataxia compared to that of the untreated Npc1-/- mice (p<0.0001). The effect on motor
function was more prominent when animals were treated pre-symptomatically than when

treatment was initiated in Npc1-/- mice after symptoms had started (p<0.003) (Figure 17
panels A and B).

Study of Pre-Symptomatic Treatment With NALL on Gait Abnormalities, Motor
Function, and Lifespan (“Survival”) of Npc1-/- Mice
The effects of levacetylleucine on motor dysfunction during ambulation were measured in
Npc1-/- mice using the Rotarod, which measures coordination and balance, and using the
CatWalk gait analysis, which measures paw placement abnormalities. As shown in Figure 18,
treatment of pre-symptomatic Npc1-/- mice had a significant improvement in hind paw duty
cycle (Figure 18 panel A) and hind paw stand mean (Figure 18 panel B) in CatWalk test
compared to untreated Npc1-/- mice (Panel A). Treatment of Npc1-/- mice with 0.1 g/kg/day
levacetylleucine also led to significant functional improvements in hind paw duty cycle
(Figure 18 panel A) and hind paw stand mean (Figure 18 panel B) in mice treated after onset
of symptoms compared to untreated Npc1-/- mice. The duty cycle is the percentage of the step
cycle spent to the stand phase versus the swing phase (i.e., duty cycle % = stand phase/step
cycle) (Haulcomb et al. 2017).

the age of 3 weeks old improved motor function as measured as time spent on the
accelerating Rotarod (starting from 1 rpm to 10 rpm, accelerating by one rpm every 30
seconds) compared to untreated Npc1-/- mice (Figure 18 panel C). Additionally, presymptomatic treatment (treatment initiated at weaning) with levacetylleucine of Npc1-/- mice
increased lifespan (+9.1%) compared to untreated Npc1-/- mice (Figure 18 panel D).

Study of Neuroinflammation in the Brain of Npc1-/- Mice
The Applicant states that neuroinflammation in the brain is a characteristic of NPC, and that
this manifests as progressive loss of cerebellar Purkinje cells in Npc1-/- mice, progressing
from the anterior to posterior lobes, and is accompanied by microglial activation. Npc1-/- mice
treated with 0.1 g/kg/day levacetylleucine in the diet from the age of 3 weeks old show a
reduction in the frequency of CD68 positive (a marker of microglial activation) cells at 59
days (about 8 weeks) old compared to untreated Npc1-/- mice (20%; p=0.077). The authors
state that this suggests a slowing of disease progression by levacetylleucine (Figure 19
panels A and B).

The Applicant cited additional publications that describe other potential modes of action
whereby NALL may improve aspects of cellular function in the context of neurodegenerative
diseases; however, the data were either not specific to NPC or were of uncertain relevance to
the proposed context of use.

13.2.2. Safety Pharmacology
The Applicant evaluated safety pharmacology endpoints in good laboratory practice (GLP)compliant stand-alone studies to evaluate effects on the central nervous system,
cardiovascular system, and respiratory system.
13.2.2.1. Central Nervous System
Study Title: Neuropharmacological screening in rats according to Irwin following oral
administration of IB1001 (Study 38192) (GLP compliant)
Methods: Effects of levacetylleucine were evaluated in female rats (8 in each group)
following administration of a single oral dose of 250, 800, or 2500 mg/kg (dose volume of
10 mL/kg). Effects were evaluated on the following 40 neuropharmacological parameters at
15-, 30-, 60- and 120-minute postdose timepoints.

138
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Table 48. Summary of Endpoints Evaluated in the Irwin Functional Observational Battery in
Rats

Source: Compiled from the Applicant’s NDA
Vehicle control was NaCl 0.9 %, w/v, pH 8.0.

Results: At oral doses of 250, 800, or 2500 mg/kg, levacetylleucine had no adverse effects on
any of the neuropharmacological parameters compared to controls. The oral NOAEL of
levacetylleucine on neuropharmacological parameters was 2500 mg/kg; however, the design
of the study was not adequately justified (e.g., the use of a single sex (females), the selection
of the high dose). Based on the single-dose Cmax exposure measured in a 26-week study
(Study 36242) in which rats were dosed at 2500 mg/kg, the safety multiple at the NOAEL
dose of 2500 mg/kg is 5- to 10-fold when compared to the respective steady-state Cmax levels
in humans.
13.2.2.2. Cardiovascular System
Study Title: IB1001: Telemetric Evaluation of Cardiovascular Effects in the Conscious
Beagle Dog (Oral Gavage Administration) (Study 8440125) (GLP compliant)
Methods: Effects of single dose of levacetylleucine were evaluated when administered by
oral gavage in telemetered beagle dogs at doses of 100, 200 or 600 mg/kg. This study was
conducted in four surgically instrumented male Beagle dogs using a Latin Square crossover
design, in which each dog received all dose levels in a randomized manner. Doses were
separated by a washout period of at least 6 days (which is greater than the 10 half-lives [t½]
of <2 hours). Endpoints evaluated included arterial blood pressure (systolic, diastolic, and
derived mean), heart rate, body temperature, ECG lead II intervals (PR interval, QRS
interval, QT, and heart rate corrected QT) and waveform morphology. The vehicle was
NaCl 0.9%, w/v, pH 8.0. The telemetric data were collected 120 min before dosing through
24 hours post dose. The measured data were averaged at hourly intervals and compared to
controls. Clinical and behavioral signs were observed as 1-, 2- and 4-hours post dose. The
design of the study was not adequately justified (e.g., the use of a single sex (males), the
selection of the high dose).
Results: Levacetylleucine, at oral doses of 100, 200 or 600 mg/kg, caused no adverse clinical
or behavioral signs, or effects on body temperature and cardiovascular parameters (blood
pressure [systolic, diastolic, and mean], heart rate, ECG intervals [PR interval, QRS interval,
QT, and heart rate corrected QT], and ECG waveform morphology) compared to vehicle
control. The oral NOAEL of levacetylleucine for cardiovascular effect was 600 mg/kg.
Based on the steady state Cmax exposure (M 61.1 μg/mL/F 83.9 μg/mL) measured in a
39-week study (Study 8422660) in which dogs were dosed at 600 mg/kg, the safety multiple
at the NOAEL dose is 8- to 12-fold when compared to the respective steady-state Cmax
(7.4 μg/mL) in humans. The dose formulation analysis indicated that the formulations met
prespecified acceptance criteria, as concentrations ranged between 95.9% and 105.0% of the
nominal concentrations and within the acceptable range of 90 to 110%.
139
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
Study Title: IB1001: Effect on human ether-a-go-go-related gene (hERG) Tail Currents
Recorded From Stably Transfected 293 human embryonic kidney (HEK293) Cells
(Study A2592) (GLP compliant)
Methods: Levacetylleucine was tested for its effects on the hERG outward tail currents
recorded in HEK293 cells stably transfected with cDNA encoding this cardiac potassium
channel. The test was performed to evaluate the potential for QT-prolongation by
levacetylleucine. The concentrations tested were at the close to the limit of solubility
(concentration of 1000μM/525 μM [nominal/actual concentration determined by analysis of
dose formulations after completion of the study]) E-4031 (a methane sulfonanilide class III
antiarrhythmic drug) was used as positive control.
Results: At the highest feasible concentration of 525μM, levacetylleucine inhibited hERG
tail currents by less than 30%, therefore, half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) could
not be determined, as the concentration response curve was limited by solubility. 525μM
(91 μg/mL) provide a more than 12-fold safety margin when compared to the mean,
steady-state Cmax level in human plasma (7.4 μg/mL). The results indicate that
levacetylleucine is not likely to inhibit the hERG channel when administered at the intended
doses.
13.2.2.3. Respiratory System
Study Title: Examination of Respiratory Parameters in Rats Following Oral Administration
of IB1001 Employing Whole Body Plethysmography (Study 38191) (GLP)
Methods: Levacetylleucine was tested at single oral doses of 250, 800 and 2500 mg/kg,
administered by oral gavage at dose volume of 10 mL/kg in female rats (8 in each group).
Respiratory endpoints (respiratory rate, tidal volume, minute volume, peak inspiratory flow,
peak expiratory flow, inspiratory time, expiratory time, and the airway resistance index) were
measured by whole-body plethysmography for 4 hours postdose (baseline was measured
predose for 1 hour). The vehicle control was NaCl 0.9 %, w/v, pH 8.0 and Carbamyl-βmethylcholine chloride (bethanechol) at 30 mg (subcutaneous) was used as positive control.
Respiratory parameters were assessed at intervals of 10 minutes for the first hour (10 minutes
to 60 minutes) and intervals of 30 minutes postdose for the remainder of the experiment
(1 hour to 4 hours).
Results: Levacetylleucine, at oral doses of 250, 800 and 2500 mg/kg, did not affect
respiratory parameters (respiratory rate, tidal volume, minute volume, peak inspiratory flow,
peak expiratory flow, inspiratory time, expiratory time, and the airway resistance index) in
female rats following administration of a single dose. The oral NOAEL of levacetylleucine
on respiratory parameters was 2500 mg/kg. Based on the single-dose Cmax exposure measured
in a 26-week study (Study 36242) in which rats were dosed at 2500 mg/kg, the safety
multiple at the NOAEL dose of 2500 mg/kg is 5- to 10-fold when compared to the respective
steady-state Cmax in humans (Table 62); however, the design of the study was not adequately
justified (e.g., the use of a single sex (females), the selection of the high dose).

Levacetylleucine was non-mutagenic in vitro (in bacterial cells) and non-clastogenic in vitro
(in cultured human peripheral lymphocytes) and in vivo (in bone marrow cells of the CD
rats).

13.2.5. Carcinogenicity
Rodent carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with levacetylleucine. PMRs for
carcinogenicity will be issued because levacetylleucine is likely to be administered for a
lifetime beginning in infancy or early childhood.

13.2.6. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
13.2.6.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
A fertility and early embryonic development study in rats has not been conducted with
levacetylleucine. PMRs for evaluating effects of levacetylleucine on fertility and early
embryonic development will be issued because there are adult-onset forms of NPC, and
treatment with levacetylleucine for early onset NPC may continue into reproductive age;
therefore, some patients of reproductive age are likely to be exposed. Additionally,
levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid and appears to have widespread distribution;
therefore, placental and lactational transfer are expected.
13.2.6.2. Embryonic and Fetal Development
Dose-ranging embryonic and fetal development studies in rats and rabbits have been
conducted with levacetylleucine. The data from the Applicant’s dose-ranging studies indicate
levacetylleucine is potentially fetotoxic and causes effects on embryofetal development in
rats and rabbits, as described below.
The Applicant conducted a 10-day oral dose-range finding study in non-pregnant rabbits at
1250 or 2500 mg/kg/day in two groups of three female rabbits New Zealand White
(HsdHra:(NZW)SPF) to determine tolerability of levacetylleucine. A decrease in food

Toxicokinetics

Cesarean Section Data
Reproductive Assessment
All phase 1 females were pregnant. On GD 13, one 1250 mg/kg/day female (Animal R0201)
died following dosing but was considered due to gavage error and not related to test article
effect.
In all levacetylleucine-treated groups, mean number of live fetuses per litter was lower and
reached statistical significance in 675 mg/kg/day and 2500 mg/kg/day groups. However,
reduction in live fetuses in low dose group is likely due to fewer implantations and not test
article-related effects. The mean number of early resorptions, mean number of total
resorptions, mean resorption rate, and mean post-implantation loss were higher in
2500 mg/kg/day than controls (Table 60).
Mean gravid uterine weight was lower than control in all levacetylleucine-treated groups and
reached statistical significance in the 2500 mg/kg/day (-39.9%). The decrease in gravid
uterine weight corelated with increase in post-implantation loss in this group.
F1 Offspring
Terminal Observations
Fetal Weights
Mean fetal weights (males, females, and the sexes combined) were lower than controls,
although not statistically significant, in a dose-responsive manner in mid (1250 mg/kg/day)
and high (2500 mg/kg/day) dose groups (Table 60).
Fetal Malformations/Variations (External, Visceral, Skeletal)
Fetal external malformations observed in 2500 mg/kg/day group included open eyes (two of
six litters), partially open eyes (three of six litters), and hyperflexion of the limbs (two of six
litters). Other external malformations were of low occurrence or lacked dose responsiveness
(Table 60).

Conclusion
In conclusion, in phase 1 animals, daily oral administration of IB1001 via gavage from GD 6
to 19 at up to 2500 mg/kg/day was maternally tolerated with decreases in food consumption
at the highest dose. Levacetylleucine appeared to be fetotoxic at doses of ≥1250 mg/kg/day
with a dose-related increase in resorptions and post-implantation loss that corresponded with
a decrease in mean maternal weight, and a slight decrease in the mean number of live fetuses
at doses greater than 675 mg/kg/day. There was also a decrease in mean fetal body weight.
Levacetylleucine induced fetal malformations in dams treated at doses of ≥1250 mg/kg/day.
Findings included fetal external malformations of open/partially open eyes and hyperflexion
of the limbs, fetal skeletal malformations of small interparietal bone and misshapen maxilla
and/or premaxilla. The adverse embryofetal findings observed at doses ≥1250 mg/kg/day
occurred in the absence of overt toxicity to the dam; therefore, N-acetyl-L-leucine is
considered a selective developmental toxicant.
13.2.6.3. Prenatal and Postnatal Development
The prenatal and postnatal development study in rats has not been conducted with
levacetylleucine. PMRs for evaluating effects of levacetylleucine on pre- and postnatal
development will be issued because onset of NPC ranges from infancy into adulthood, and
treatment with levacetylleucine may range from infancy into late adulthood. Levacetylleucine
was found to cause increases in post-implantation loss and malformation in skeletal bones in
a dose-range finding (DRF) EFD study in rabbits. Leucine, a metabolite of levacetylleucine,
injection as a single 0.2 mL of a 1% solution in the amnionic sac of a 9-day-old embryo of
chicken is reported to cause morphological anomalies of the legs (including inward flexion of
the toes, leg extension or flexion at hip joint), defects in ossification of the skull, torsion of
the neck and depression of the brain waves (Bergstrom et al. 1967). Therefore, exposure to
levacetylleucine has potential to cause adverse effects on growth and development
(neurological, physical, and sexual) from prenatal and into postnatal stage of life and requires
investigation. Additionally, levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid and appears to have
widespread distribution; therefore, placental and lactational transfer are expected.
13.2.6.4. Juvenile Animal Studies
A DRF juvenile toxicity study was conducted in postnatal day (PND) 7-PND 28 Wistar Han
rats (six animals/sex/group) with levacetylleucine at doses 300, 600, 1000 mg/kg
administered once daily (QD) by oral gavage. The animal was observed for survival, clinical
and post dose observations, body weights and food consumption. At termination animals
were observed for macroscopic changes. A dose of 1000 mg/kg/day was found to be tolerated
without significant toxicological findings in any of the above parameters.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
Protein Binding
The plasma protein binding of levacetylleucine was assessed in plasma from CD1 mice,
Sprague-Dawley rats, New Zealand White rabbits, Beagle dogs, and humans at
concentrations of 1 μg/mL, 10 μg/mL, and 100 μg/mL using rapid equilibrium dialysis
(Study No. ADM-20-2970b, 2020). The analysis was performed using a specially developed
liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry method, with all samples compared
against a calibration curve prepared in a corresponding matrix. The results showed that the
free fractions of levacetylleucine were approximately 100% across all species and
concentrations, indicating minimal binding to plasma proteins.
Metabolism
Study No. ADM-20-2970a, 2020
In this study, a deuterated N-acetyl-DL-leucine was used to compare metabolism across
various species (human, mouse, rat, rabbit, dog) at concentrations of 40 μM, 2mM, and 1, 10,
and 100μM. The study found that only one metabolite, L-leucine, was produced during a
1-hour incubation period, regardless of the species. The highest metabolic turnover of
N-acetyl-DL-leucine-D10 was observed in rabbit liver S9, with 39 to 51% of the initial
concentration remaining after 60 minutes. In comparison, mouse, rat, and human liver S9
showed a remaining concentration range of 47 to 72%. The lowest disappearance rate was
seen in dog liver S9, where 85% or more of the parent molecule remained after 60 minutes.
The disappearance of N-acetyl-DL-leucine-D10 was not cofactor-dependent, indicating it was
not mediated by cytochrome P450 (CYP), uridine diphosphate glucuronosyltransferase, or
sulfotransferase enzymes.
No qualitative differences in metabolism were observed between the two doses across any
species, indicating consistent metabolic capacity across species. The measured concentrations
of L-leucine-D10 correlated well with the disappearance of N-acetyl-DL-leucine-D10,
suggesting that the observed disappearance is primarily due to the formation of L-leucineD10.
Excretion
No specific excretion studies have been conducted for levacetylleucine. However, since its
metabolism produces only acetate and L-leucine, both of which are further processed in the
body through catabolic pathways, dedicated excretion studies were deemed unnecessary. Lee
et al. 2015 showed that after administering an intravenous dose of 14C-L-leucine to rats,
5% of the parent compound was excreted in urine as levacetylleucine.
Cytochrome P450 Induction
Study No. ADM-18-2066a, 2018
The induction potential of levacetylleucine on human CYP enzymes 1A2, 2B6, and 3A4 at
the mRNA level was evaluated using cryopreserved hepatocytes from three human donors at
concentrations ranging from 6 to 2000 µg/mL [Study No. ADM-18-2066a, 2018]. The total
169
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
exposure duration was 48 hours, with the cell culture media containing the test compound
being refreshed after 24 hours. Cytotoxicity was assessed visually and by measuring lactate
dehydrogenase leakage after 24 hours of incubation.
The mRNA levels of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 following incubation with
levacetylleucine at concentrations of 6 to 2000 µg/mL remained within 0.5- to 1.7-fold of the
solvent control and below 3% of the corresponding positive control inducer. These results
indicate that N-acetyl-L-leucine did not induce these genes. Additionally, no signs of
cytotoxicity or morphological changes were observed due to the test compound.
Transporter Inhibition
P-gp, BCRP, and BSEP
Study No. ADM-18-2106a, 2018
Efflux transporter inhibition was assessed by measuring the vesicular uptake of a probe
substrate into inside-out membrane vesicles expressing individual human adenosine
triphosphate (ATP) binding cassette transporters (P-glycoprotein [P-gp], breast cancer
resistance protein [BCRP], and bile salt export pump [BSEP]) in the presence and absence of
levacetylleucine (MW=173.21 g/mol) at six concentrations ranging from 6 µg/mL to
2000 µg/mL, both with and without ATP [Study No. ADM-18-2106a, 2018]. Inhibition of the
transporters was indicated by a reduced level of vesicular uptake of the probe substrate in the
presence of ATP.
The Applicant demonstrated that levacetylleucine inhibited the efflux transporters P-gp,
BCRP, and BSEP, with IC50 values of 227 µg/mL (1.31mM) for P-gp, 491 µg/mL (2.83mM)
for BCRP, and 408 µg/mL (2.36mM) for BSEP.
Based on the molecular weight of levacetylleucine (173.21 g/mol) and its IC50 values for key
efflux transporters, potential drug interactions have been assessed. With a maximum dose of
2 grams, the calculated Igut/IC50 ratio is close to 10 for BCRP, but a very high ratio of 35.2 for
P-gp was observed. This ratio suggests a potential for interaction with substrates of P-gp. The
FDA has recommended a PMR study to further investigate these potential interactions.
OATP1B1*1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, and OCT2
Study No. ADM-18-2106c, 2018
Human solute carrier transporter inhibition was evaluated by measuring the cellular uptake of
probe substrates into HEK293 cells (Corning® TransportoCells™) overexpressing a single
human solute carrier transporter (organic anion transporter [OAT]P1B1*1, OATP1B3,
OAT1, OAT3, or organic cation transporter [OCT]2) in the absence and presence of
levacetylleucine (MW=173.21 g/mol) at concentrations ranging from 30 to 900 µg/mL
[Study No. ADM-18-2106c, 2018]. Inhibition of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 was evaluated
both with and without a 30-minute pre-incubation of the cells with the test compound.
Inhibition of active transport was indicated by decreased cellular uptake of the probe
substrate in the presence of the inhibitor. Positive control inhibitors for each transporter were
incubated in parallel to confirm active cellular uptake of the probe substrate.
Inhibition of OAT1 and OAT3 by levacetylleucine was observed, with IC50 values of
1030 µg/mL (5.9mM) for OAT1 and 103 µg/mL (0.59mM) for OAT3. Additionally, minor
inhibition of OCT2 was observed, with 61% activity remaining at 900 µg/mL

170
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
levacetylleucine (extrapolated IC50 of 1700 µg/mL). Levacetylleucine was not an inhibitor of
OATP1B1*1 or OATP1B3.
Based on these results, inhibition of the human solute carrier transporters OATP1B1*1,
OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, or OCT2 by levacetylleucine is considered unlikely.
Transporter Substrate
P-gp and BCRP
The apparent permeability of levacetylleucine was measured bidirectionally (pH 7.4; apical to
basolateral [A>B] and basolateral to apical [B>A]) at a concentration of 10 µg/mL through
Madin-Darby canine kidney (MDCK)-wild type, MDCK-BCRP, and MDCK-P-gp cell
monolayers. Incubations with MDCK-BCRP and MDCK-P-gp cells were conducted both in
the presence and absence of specific chemical inhibitors for BCRP and P-gp, respectively
[Study No. ADM-18-2106d, 2018]. Appropriate positive and negative controls were
included. Transporter inhibition was indicated by a reduced level of vesicular uptake of the
probe substrate in the presence of ATP.
The efflux ratio for levacetylleucine was 1.6 with MDCK-wild type cells (consistent across
both plates), 0.7 with MDCK-BCRP without inhibitor and 1.3 with the inhibitor (2 μM
Ko143), and 1.6 with MDCK-P-gp without inhibitor and 2.6 with the inhibitor (50μM
verapamil). Therefore, no BCRP- or P-gp-mediated active transport of N-acetyl-L-leucine
was detected in this study.
OAT1, OAT3, and OCT2
The cellular uptake of levacetylleucine was measured in human cells transfected with single
transporters at two concentrations (10 and 100 µg/mL) both in the presence and absence of
chemical transporter inhibitors, as well as in control cells without transfected transporters and
without inhibitors [Study No. ADM-18-2106e, 2018]. Transporter-mediated uptake was
identified by comparing the cellular uptake in transporter overexpressing cells to that in
vector-transfected control cells and by observing the inhibition of uptake in transporter
overexpressing cells when control inhibitors were used. Positive control substrates at a single
concentration were tested in parallel.
Levacetylleucine was found to be a substrate for OAT1 and OAT3, but not OCT2, in vitro.
Uptake into OAT1-expressing cells was approximately 5-fold higher, and into OAT3expressing cells approximately 9-fold higher, after 3 minutes of incubation compared to
control cells. This uptake was completely inhibited by the addition of diclofenac.
The clinical relevance of this finding is unknown. However, due to the overall safety profile
of levacetylleucine, no significant drug interactions with inhibitors of OAT1 or OAT3
transporters are expected.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
Trial IB1001-201
Pharmacokinetic (PK) analysis for Trial IB1001-201 was conducted on the PK set, which
included all subjects who received at least one dose of levacetylleucine in the extension phase
(EP) and had at least one PK plasma concentration measurement after oral administration.
Intensive PK samples were collected at Visit 7B, following the first administration of the
drug, and again after 1 year of treatment (Visit 9B). The Applicant conducted non171
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
compartment analysis using the intensive PK data. Sparse PK sampling from the parent study
was not included in this context but was instead used for the population PK (PopPK) analysis.
At Visit 7B, the highest mean plasma concentration of the drug was observed 1 hour after
administration, with a mean (±standard deviation [SD]) Cmax value of 6.69 (±1.85) μg/mL,
and a Cmax value of 10.04 µg/mL (Table 63). At Visit 9B, the highest mean plasma
concentration was again reached 1-hour post-treatment, with a mean (±SD) Cmax value of
8.32 (±3.3 SD) μg/mL and a Cmax value of 15.85 µg/mL (Table 64). However, geometric
coefficient of variation values at all time points exceeded 40%, indicating substantial
variability in plasma concentrations among subjects.
When administered orally, levacetylleucine was rapidly absorbed, with time to maximum
concentration values ranging from 0.5 to 2.5 hours and a median value of 1 hour. The halflife (t1/2) was approximately 1 hour. Likely metabolites include L-leucine and acetic acid.
Elimination of levacetylleucine from plasma was rapid after a single dose. The apparent
volume of distribution was 200 L, and the apparent clearance was 144 L/h. Similar values
were observed at steady state. A large inter-individual variability (coefficient of variation:
40 to 50%) was observed at both Visit 7B and Visit 9B. No relevant sex differences were
observed in any of the PK parameters. PK parameters for visit 7B are summarized
descriptively in (Table 63) and PK parameters for visit 9B are summarized descriptively in
(Table 64).

Trial IB1001-301 – PK Results
At the time when action was taken on initial NDA submission, the IB1001-301 EP is still
ongoing, and therefore data from the rich PK sampling are not yet available. No
noncompartmental PK analysis was performed on data from the sparse PK sampling from the
parent study (Trial IB1001-301 PK report); instead, these samples were utilized in the PopPK
analysis.
Impact of Food Effect on Pharmacokinetics
The Applicant did not conduct dedicated food effect study using the to-be-marketed
formulation. In Trial IB1001-301, drug administration was proposed to be under fasted
conditions (at least 30 minutes before meal or at least 2 hours after meal). However,
approximately 12.5% non-compliance with fasting was reported. The food components (high
173
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
fat versus low fat) were unknown. The review team conducted preliminary PK analyses to
compare the drug concentration at various time points (0.5-, 1-, 1.5-, and 2-hour) following
drug administrations under two scenarios: with and without meal intake. The results (Figure
23 and Table 67) show that the drug concentrations are similar between the groups that
consumed a meal and those that did not across all time points. While there is some variability
within each group, the overall pattern indicates that meal intake did not significantly affect
drug concentration during the first 2 hours postdose. These data suggest that given the current
available information, the drug's absorption and subsequent concentrations remain similar
regardless of meal intake.

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment–Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
No studies of immunogenicity and its impact on PK/PD, efficacy and safety were conducted.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
Population PK Analysis
Applicant’s Population PK Analysis
The PopPK analysis for levacetylleucine was conducted based on the PK data collected from
three trials: IB1001-201 (n=32), IB1001-202 (n=25), and IB1001-301 (n=38). Sparse PK
sampling was collected in Trials IB1001-201, IB1001-202 and IB1001-301 and rich PK
sampling was conducted in the EPs of Trials IB1001-201 and IB1001-202. A total of 567
levacetylleucine concentrations from 95 subjects were used in PopPK analysis. In the PopPK
population, majority of subjects were White (94%) and male (51%). The median (range) age was
26 (6, 67) years old, and the median (range) body weight was 59.2 (20.5, 98.4) kg. Dosing
regimen used in IB1001-201 is same as the proposed dosing regimen with minor deviations in
the age categories for dosing in IB1001-201 and IB1001-202.
Phoenix WinNonlinTM nonlinear mixed effect using estimation method of First Order
Conditional Estimation-Extended Least Squares (FOCE ELS) was used for PopPK analysis. To
investigate the source of between-subject variability, intrinsic factors and extrinsic factors were
tested for inclusion in the base model using forward stepwise covariate search to test the addition
or deletion of covariates effects by adding one at a time. Visual predictive check and bootstrap
test were used to validate the final covariate model.

186
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
The base model is a one-compartment model with the error model combining additive and
multiplicative method. The base model also includes a 5-minute lag time (Tlag) and covariance
between clearance (CL) and volume of distribution (V). Between-subject variability coefficient
of variation were 39% for CL and 74% for V. The examined covariates were age, sex, weight,
body mass index, body surface area, disease (NPC or GM2 gangliosidosis), race (White, Asian
or other), dose (1 gram or 2 grams), miglustat as comedication, and meal consumption (no meal,
meal consumed during the 2 hours before drug administration, meal consumed during the 2
hours after drug administration, meal consumed during the 2 hours both before and after the drug
administration). The final PopPK model includes the effect of body weight with power model on
CL and V, and the effect of dose (1 gram versus 2 grams) on the absorption rate constant (Ka).
The final model estimates are presented in Table 82. Goodness-of-fit plots and visual predictive
check are presented in Figure 24 and Figure 25. Between-subject variability coefficient of
variation were 29% for CL, 80% for V, and 19% for Ka. Eta shrinkages were 20%, 44%, and
48% for CL, V, and Ka.

Reviewer’s Assessment
Data handling of exclusions and correction of missing values and unplausible values were
adequately documented. The final PopPK model reasonably describes the observed PK data. The
reviewer’s independent analysis shows similar parameter estimates as Applicant’s. The structural
parameters were estimated with acceptable precision (relative standard error <20%). The
goodness-of-fit plots does not show obvious model misspecification, and the visual predictive
check plots shows that the model generally captures the central tendency and variability of the
observed data. No systemic trends were observed in ETA-covariate plots (Figure 26). Age was
not identified as significant covariate once body weight is included in the model. Eta shrinkage
for CL less than 20% supports use of empiric Bayes estimates to derive individual CL and
exposure metrics of AUC. Given the fast absorption with a short half-life and the moderately
high eta shrinkage for V, the model derived Cmax was only used for the purpose of comparisons
with the model derived Cmax values predicted for different body weight groups when evaluating
dosing regimens.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

Source: Reviewer’s analysis
Abbreviations: 0 = Yes, 1 = No; 2 = NA; BSA = body surface area; SEXF = sex, 0-male and 1-female; COVDIS = disease as
covariate, 1-NPC and 2-GM2; CL = clearance; COVMIG = comedication with miglustat; V = volume of distribution

Applicant’s PopPK analysis assumed that levacetylleucine follows dose-linear PK. There is no
dose-proportionality information provided by Applicant. The available PK data used for PopPK
analysis are limited to evaluate relative oral bioavailability (1 gram versus 2 grams). The subjects
younger than 13 years old and weighing <35 kg received 1 gram at the time of PK sampling and
the remaining subjects (≥13 years old or ≥35 kg) received 2 grams at the time of PK sampling.
Since PK samples were collected only after the morning dose, there are no data where two dose
strengths (1 gram and 2 grams) were tested within a subject or in similar subjects in terms of
body size (e.g., weight) at the time of PK sampling. This exclusive correlation between body
190
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
weight and dose levels does not allow covariate analysis to assess independent impacts of body
size (e.g., weight) and dose level (1 gram versus 2 grams) on PK parameters, such as apparent
clearance and relative bioavailability. If dose-proportionality data become available in the future,
the PopPK model should be re-assessed accordingly.
(b) (4)

Exposure-Response Analysis
Reviewer conducted E-R analysis for Agency-recommended rescored scale for the assessment
and rating of ataxia (SARA) (functional SARA) based on the data from 38 subjects who had both
efficacy evaluation and evaluable PK data in Trial IB1001-301. The exposure metric used for
E-R analysis was daily AUC at steady state derived based on the individual predicted PK
parameters (empirical bayes estimates) following the dosing regimen received during phase 3
study. No apparent E-R relationship for efficacy endpoint was observed (Figure 27). Due to the
limited event rate for each treatment-emergent adverse event (Section 7.6), E-R analysis for
safety was not performed.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Trial IB1001-201
16.1.1. Criteria To Select Primary Anchor Test for CICS
The procedures for selecting the primary anchor test were defined in an administration and
scoring manual for clinical impression of change in severity (CI-CS) Version 3, dated
January 21, 2020. At Visit 1, the Investigating Physician selected either the 8-meter walk test
(8MWT) or the 9-Hole Peg Test of the dominant hand (9HPT-D) for each subject based on the
subject’s performance at Visit 1 on the 8MWT, the Gait subtest of the SARA, the 9HPT-D, and a
cognitive assessment. Examples of standard cognitive assessments include the Montreal
Cognitive Assessment or the Mini-Mental State Examination.
The default selection of the 8MWT or 9HPT-D was based on the following criteria:
The 8MWT was selected as the default anchor test if the subject’s:
•

Score on the SARA Gait subtest was 2 to 7, or

•

Time on the 9HPT-D was <20 seconds or >150 seconds.

The 9HPT-D was selected as the default anchor test if the subject’s:
•

Score on the SARA Gait subtest was 0, 1, or 8, or

•

Time on the 9HPT-D was ≥20 seconds and ≤150 seconds.

If the subject’s score on the SARA Gait subtest was 2 to 7, and the time on the 9HPT-D was
≥20 seconds and ≤150 seconds, the Investigating Physician had to determine whether
improvement in functional aspect of gait or upper arm extremity were more clinically
meaningful for the subject. In addition, when selecting the primary anchor test, the Investigating
Physician could elicit from subjects and their family/caregivers which was their preferred or
most relevant outcome for seeing improvement.

196
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5451862

NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

16.1.2. Other Efficacy Endpoints
The key secondary endpoints for the parent study of Trial IB1001-201 were as follows:
•

The individual components of the primary endpoint (CI-CS from Visit 2 to Visit 4 and CI-CS
from Visit 4 to Visit 6).

•

Difference in the blinded rater’s Clinical Impression of Severity values from baseline to end
of treatment and from the end of treatment to the end of washout.

•

Measurement of change in performance of the CI-CS score of either the 9HPT-D or the
8MWT reclassified to a 3-point scale.

•

An evaluation of the CI-CS for the test (9HPT-D or 8MWT) that was not selected as the
primary anchor test.

Secondary endpoints for the parent study of Trial IB1001-201 were as follows:
•

Spinocerebellar ataxia functional index (SCAFI)

•

SARA

•

European Quality of Life, 5-Dimension, 5-Level for subjects aged ≥18; European Quality of
Life, 5-Dimension, Youth for subjects aged 6 to 17 years (2 parts: the European Quality of
Life visual analogue scale and the European Quality of Life descriptive system)

•

Modified disability rating scale (mDRS)

•

Clinical global impression scale of severity (CGI-S) by the treating physician; clinical global
impression scale of severity by the caregiver; clinical global impression scale of severity by
the subject

•

Clinical Global Impression of Change by the treating physician; Clinical Global Impression
of Change by the caregiver; Clinical Global Impression of Change by the subject

SARA
For the modified intent-to-treat population, the mean change in SARA total score during the
treatment period (Visit 4 versus Visit 2) was -1.2 (SD: 2), indicating an improvement in SARA
score with levacetylleucine. The mean change during the washout period (Visit 6 versus Visit 4)
was 1.4 (SD: 2.6), indicating a deterioration in the subjects’ condition at the end of the washout
period.

Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)

16.2. Trial IB1001-301: Other Efficacy Endpoints
SCAFI
The SCAFI is composed of a timed 8MWT to be carried out at maximum speed, the 9-Hole Peg
Test (which evaluates both the dominant and non-dominant hand), and the performance auditoryperceptual assessment rate to evaluate speech performance. The 8MWT and 9-Hole Peg Test are
measured in 0.1 second, and a lower time indicates a clinical improvement. The performance
auditory-perceptual assessment test evaluates the severity of dysarthria, and higher score
indicates a clinical improvement. After the assessment, raw scores were transformed into
reciprocals and converted into subtest Z-scores. The final SCAFI was then generated as the
arithmetic mean of all 3 Z-scores. Following the primary analysis method, the estimated
treatment difference between levacetylleucine and placebo in SCAFI total score was 0.07 (95%
confidence interval [CI]: 0.0, 0.15), indicating more improvement in the levacetylleucine
treatment period.
mDRS
The mDRS consists of 6 domains (ambulation, manipulation, seizures, language, swallowing,
and ocular movements) and the scale ranges from 0 to 24, where 0 is the best neurological status
and 24 is the worst. The mDRS composite score is calculated as the sum of the 6 components
divided by 24, so is on a 0 to 1 scale.
In the intent-to-treat population, the mean baseline mDRS composite score was 0.44 (SD 1.7) in
the levacetylleucine-placebo sequence and was 0.52 (SD 0.11) in the placebo-levacetylleucine
sequence. The estimated treatment difference using the primary analysis method was -0.02 (95%
CI: -0.04, -0.00), indicating improvement in mDRS composite score with levacetylleucine
compared to placebo.
5DNPCCSS
The 5-domain Niemann-Pick disease type C clinical severity scale sum score is defined as a
composite score of 5 major domains (ambulation, speech, swallow, fine motor skills, and
cognition) in Niemann-Pick disease type C clinical severity scale. Scoring of each domain ranges
from 0 to 5. A lower score indicates a clinical improvement. See Section 16.3 for a scoring form.
The estimated treatment difference based on the mixed model for repeated measures used in the
primary analysis was -0.3 (95% CI: -0.74, 0.15) for the mean change from baseline in 5-domain
Niemann-Pick disease type C clinical severity scale total score (Table 86).

within-subject comparison favored levacetylleucine.

Correlations Between SARA, mSARA, and fSARA Total Scores
Both the functional scale for the assessment and rating of ataxia (fSARA) and the modified scale
for the assessment and rating of ataxia (mSARA) are based on the modification to the original
SARA. Using all available visits for 60 randomized subjects, the estimated Spearman's rank
correlation coefficient (Spearman’s 𝜌) between SARA total score and fSARA total score was
0.94 (p-value <0.001). The estimated Spearman correlation between mSARA total score and
fSARA total score was 0.89 (p-value <0.001). The estimated Spearman correlation between
mSARA total score and SARA total score was 0.98 (p-value <0.001).
Correlations Between SARA, mDRS, and NPC-CSS in Ambulation and Speech Domains
The ambulation and speech domains of mDRS and NPC-CSS are measured in a similar manner
to the SARA. Table 88 demonstrates that the three instruments are significantly correlated in
total and domain-specific scores among 58 subjects who completed Visit 2, Visit 4, and Visit 6
assessments.

22. Data Integrity–Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
Trial IB1001-301 was an adequate and well-controlled trial and Trial IB1001-201 provided data
that served as CE. Three clinical investigators, Drs. Gascon-Bayarri, Bremova-Ertl, and
Martakis, were inspected in this NDA. During the clinical investigator inspections, source
records documenting the following were reviewed and verified against the Applicant’s data line
listings:
•

For Protocol IB1001-201: for all 10 participants enrolled by Dr. Gascon-Bayarri, the treating
physician’s CI-CS scores for the 9HPT-D or 8MWT and the central blinded rater’s CI-CS
scores for 9HPT-D or 8MWT (i.e., primary efficacy endpoint).

•

For Protocol IB1001-301: for all 12 randomized participants at Dr. Bremova-Ertl’s site and
for all 9 randomized participants at Dr. Martakis’ site, the mean change of mSARA
(i.e., primary efficacy endpoint) and the original SARA (i.e., key secondary efficacy
endpoint).

No discrepancies were noted. However, a data reliability concern was noted in the Visit 6
(b) (6)
mSARA and SARA assessment for Subject

6 ADVERSE REACTIONS

Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to
Applicant’s Draft PI
• Added a description of the overall clinical trial database from which the
adverse reaction data were drawn to include duration of exposure to
AQNEURSA (mean SD and range) with a cross-reference to Section
14 for full details.
• Revised Table 2 to include adverse reactions during Treatment Period
1 occurring in 2 or more patients.
(b) (4)
•
only adverse reactions causally
related to the drug were included.

(b) (4)

• Included laboratory finding of thrombocytopenia even though
thrombocytopenia may be due to underlying disease and/or miglustat
use because there were more subjects with thrombocytopenia on
AQNEURSA than placebo during the trial.
7 DRUG INTERACTIONS
8 USE IN SPECIFIC
POPULATIONS (e.g.,
Pregnancy, Lactation,
Females and Males of
Reproductive Potential,
Pediatric Use, Geriatric
Use, Renal Impairment,
Hepatic Impairment)

• Added subsection 7.2 to include the effect of AQNEURSA on other
drugs.
8.1 Pregnancy
• Revised Applicant-proposed language to align with Pregnancy and
Lactation Labeling Rule.
• Added embryofetal risk under Risk Summary based on animal
reproduction studies in pregnant rats and rabbits.
• Added header for Animal Data and included information on skeletal
malformations seen from dose-range finding rat and rabbit embryofetal development studies.
8.2 Lactation
• Revised Applicant proposed language to align with Pregnancy and
Lactation Labeling Rule.
8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential
• Added header for Pregnancy Testing and included information that for
females of reproductive potential, verification that patient is not
pregnant should occur.
• Added header for Contraception and included information that females
of reproductive potential should use effective contraception during
treatment with AQNEURSA and for 7 days after the last dose if DRUG
is discontinued.
8.4 Pediatric Use
• Revised Applicant proposed language to align with the Guidance for
Industry: Pediatric Information Incorporated Into Human Prescription
Drug and Biological Product Labeling (March 2019).
8.5 Geriatric Use
• Revised Applicant proposed language to align with the Draft Guidance
for Industry: Geriatric Information in Human Prescription Drug and
Biological Product Labeling (September 2020).
